- Our Consumer Society: Kind: captions Language: en-US We live in a consumer society. Our lives revolve&nbsp; around shops, supermarkets, new experiences, new&nbsp;&nbsp; stuff, a world surrounded by image, advertising,&nbsp; marketing, one-day delivery, product placement,&nbsp;&nbsp; gadgets and fast fashion. We are – supposedly -&nbsp; shallow, obsessed with the self, narcissistic and&nbsp;&nbsp; vein, building personal castles of consumption&nbsp; on the foundations of we don’t really need. But is this true? In some sense, of course,&nbsp; we’ve always consumed. And while we do&nbsp;&nbsp; consume the latest frivolous fashion&nbsp; and want the latest pointless gadget,&nbsp;&nbsp; we also consume meaningful things -&nbsp; new foods and books, travel and art. So consumerism is a broad and vague concept. From&nbsp; the latin – consumere – it used to mean to use&nbsp;&nbsp; up and was limited to candles, food, drink,&nbsp; wood – things that you would literally consume,&nbsp;&nbsp; would disappear. But its obvious that throughout&nbsp; the modern period and increasingly from the&nbsp;&nbsp; 1950s, something has changed. Something&nbsp; new has happened. A consumer revolution,&nbsp;&nbsp; maybe? Consumption expanded, across the&nbsp; globe, across the classes. It began to&nbsp;&nbsp; dominate our lives. At some point we&nbsp; transformed into a consumer society. The consumer society is different from previous&nbsp; forms of consumption. We have a wider variety&nbsp;&nbsp; of goods and shops, more pervasive marketing,&nbsp; everything is commercialised and exchanged, and&nbsp;&nbsp; consumerism has become not just about producing&nbsp; new goods, but has become a cultural phenomenon. In his classic 1993 book Land of Desire,&nbsp;&nbsp; historian William Leach says that "The&nbsp; cardinal features of this culture were&nbsp;&nbsp; acquisition and consumption as the means of&nbsp; achieving happiness; the cult of the new;&nbsp;&nbsp; the democratisation of desire; and money value as&nbsp; the predominant measure of all value in society.’ And historian Peter Sterns defines consumerism&nbsp;&nbsp; as: ‘a society in which many people formulate&nbsp; their goals in life partly through acquiring&nbsp;&nbsp; goods that they clearly do not need for&nbsp; subsistence or for traditional display.’ So today, I want to explore our consumer world,&nbsp; our landscape of desire. What does consumerism&nbsp;&nbsp; mean? Is the term even useful? Is the West&nbsp; ‘shallow’? What does it do to us to be surrounded&nbsp;&nbsp; by image, advertising, and celebrity? Is&nbsp; consumerism making us ill, alienating us from our&nbsp;&nbsp; true selves? Is there any possibility of ethical&nbsp; or conscious consumption? Is there a way out? First, we need to look at the history of how&nbsp; consumerism developed, what we might have&nbsp;&nbsp; lost and when and why this change happened, to&nbsp; really understand what our consumer society is. Modern consumerism, as we understand it&nbsp; today, began to develop in the city states&nbsp;&nbsp; of renaissance Italy in the 15th century. Trade&nbsp; between the Far East, Middle East, and Europe&nbsp;&nbsp; increased, merchants became wealthier,&nbsp; and disposable income became more common. Artisans and shopkeepers traded&nbsp; in, owned, and collected increasing&nbsp;&nbsp; numbers of household wares like jars,&nbsp; bowls, plates, cutlery and textiles like&nbsp;&nbsp; clothes or curtains. Carvings and tapestries and&nbsp; art became more elaborate and more widespread. But the new economics of consumption&nbsp; – the newfound wealth of merchants&nbsp;&nbsp; exploring the globe - developed&nbsp; alongside an already existing moral&nbsp;&nbsp; economy in Italy that questioned consumerisms&nbsp; benefits and sought to moderate its excesses. Aristotle – whose works became more widely read&nbsp;&nbsp; during the renaissance – had argued that the&nbsp; displaying of wealth should be done virtuously.&nbsp;&nbsp; A wealthy man, he said, to be admired, should&nbsp; contribute to public works and communal feasts.&nbsp;&nbsp; Brash undirected luxury was&nbsp; immoral. It had no social use. For Plato, too, luxury led to laziness,&nbsp;&nbsp; corruption, and an inward drive for&nbsp; every increasing selfish desires. The ancient Stoics also rejected the idea of&nbsp; becoming attached to anything material and&nbsp;&nbsp; fleeting. Identifying with and becoming attached&nbsp; to an external world outside of your control would&nbsp;&nbsp; only lead to frustration when those attachments&nbsp; were challenged, perished, or were taken away.&nbsp;&nbsp; The Ancient Greeks, for the most&nbsp; part, had emphasized the inner life. The Christian Church, which of course had&nbsp; a strong presence in Renaissance Italy,&nbsp;&nbsp; adopted the values of these&nbsp; Ancient Greek philosophies. And so many in places like Florence,&nbsp; Venice and Milan were sceptical of&nbsp;&nbsp; this new commercial culture. The newly&nbsp; wealthy merchants had to ensure that&nbsp;&nbsp; their possessions were consistent with the&nbsp; conservative sensibilities of the time. In 1497, one friar started a ‘bonfire of&nbsp; vanities’ in the middle of Florence – board games,&nbsp;&nbsp; instruments, cosmetics, clothes and tapestries&nbsp; – anything that could tempt one to sin&nbsp;&nbsp; -were all burned as citizens&nbsp; danced around the bonfire. Venetians passed laws trying to regulate&nbsp; luxury, taxing spending at weddings and&nbsp;&nbsp; prohibiting certain dress styles, and&nbsp; tapestries more that 1.5m in length. As commerce thrived across Europe,&nbsp; other European states followed. In England, for example, wearing purple&nbsp; silk and gold was restricted to royals.&nbsp;&nbsp; In London in 1574, a tailor&nbsp; was sent to prison for wearing&nbsp;&nbsp; a ‘pair of hose lined with taffety, and a shirt&nbsp; edged wieth silver contrary to the ordinances’ While In much of Asia, the Buddhist tradition,&nbsp;&nbsp; much like the stoics, rejected the&nbsp; idea of attachment with possessions. It was only in the 18th century that&nbsp; these attitudes began to change. As global trade increased, people began collecting&nbsp; new and unique candlesticks, exotic linens and&nbsp;&nbsp; textiles, spices, tankards, birdcages,&nbsp; porcelains, stockings, pipes, and soaps. A taste for new foods and drinks – made&nbsp; cheaper and more abundant by the Atlantic slave&nbsp;&nbsp; trade – increased and consumers became addicted&nbsp; to coffee, tea, chocolate, and especially sugar. Cotton from plants – again, made cheaper with&nbsp; slave labour - instead of animals were cheaper,&nbsp;&nbsp; took dyes better and suddenly clothes&nbsp; became more colourful and more varied. All of this coincided with the&nbsp; development of modern marketing,&nbsp;&nbsp; advertising, and salesmanship as merchants&nbsp; sought new ways to promote new goods. English pottery maker Josiah Wedgwood invented&nbsp; the modern salesman in the 18th century. As historian Neil McKendrick writes Wedgwood used: ‘inertia-selling campaigns, product&nbsp; differentiation, market segmentation,&nbsp;&nbsp; detailed market research, embryonic self-service&nbsp; schemes, money-back-if-not-satisfied policies,&nbsp;&nbsp; free carriage, giveaway sales promotions,&nbsp; auctions, lotteries, catalogues… advanced credit,&nbsp;&nbsp; three-tier discount schemes, including major&nbsp; discounts for first orders, and almost every&nbsp;&nbsp; form of advertisement, trade cards, shop signs,&nbsp; letterheads, bill heads, newspaper and magazine&nbsp;&nbsp; advertisements, fashion plates and fashion&nbsp; magazines, solicited puffs, organized propaganda&nbsp;&nbsp; campaigns, even false attacks organized to produce&nbsp; the opportunity to publicize the counterattack ‘ Throughout the 19th century consumerism&nbsp; continued entering the home.&nbsp;&nbsp; In Britain, a new trend - Wallpaper led&nbsp; to sales going through the roof. Art,&nbsp;&nbsp; photography, and miniature replicas&nbsp; of statues and ornaments became&nbsp;&nbsp; cheaper to reproduce and manufacture.&nbsp; Clocks and watches became more popular. Then, glass windows were invented. Shop windows, glass cabinets and displays created&nbsp; a new relationship between the consumer good&nbsp;&nbsp; and the consumer. ‘Perhaps more than any other&nbsp; medium,’the historian JW Lungstrum writes, ‘glass&nbsp;&nbsp; democratized desire even as it&nbsp; democratized access to goods.’ And in the 20th century,&nbsp; DIY, tools, commercial music,&nbsp;&nbsp; radio, cinema, and television, technology&nbsp; and gadgets and computers supercharged the&nbsp;&nbsp; consumer revolution. McDonald opened its doors&nbsp; in 1955. Credit cards were introduced in 1958.&nbsp;&nbsp; Comic books and Hollywood, Microsoft,&nbsp; and Apple, took over the world. Supermarkets and shopping malls sprang up and&nbsp; governments invested in highways to connect them.&nbsp;&nbsp; In 1946 there were just 8 suburban&nbsp; shopping centres in the US,&nbsp;&nbsp; 14 years later in 1960 there were almost 4000. These new cathedrals of commerce&nbsp; epitomised the shift in culture.&nbsp;&nbsp; Famed architect Victor Gruen designed the largest&nbsp; shopping mall in the world in Detroit in 1952.&nbsp;&nbsp; It had a hundred stores, private roads, a&nbsp; power plant, a police force, a water system,&nbsp;&nbsp; and was designed as a self-contained city. Gruen’s&nbsp; next project in Minnesota was even more ambitious.&nbsp;&nbsp; He designed it so that deliveries, the organising&nbsp; of stock, the wires & pipes were accessed from the&nbsp;&nbsp; back, hidden from view, so that everything&nbsp; appeared on the shelves as if by magic. Gruen believed that these new developments could&nbsp; be the hearts of the new suburban communities&nbsp;&nbsp; that were springing up, that were in desperate&nbsp; need of a kind of spiritual communal core. Later&nbsp;&nbsp; in life he became appalled at how they turned&nbsp; out. He wrote that instead of providing the “need,&nbsp;&nbsp; place and opportunity for participation in&nbsp; modern community life that the ancient Greek&nbsp;&nbsp; Agora, the Medieval Market and Place, and&nbsp; our own Town Squares provided in the past,”&nbsp;&nbsp; they’d become ‘bastard developments’. He&nbsp; retired to Vienna, becoming a science fiction&nbsp;&nbsp; commenting on the environmental destruction&nbsp; our new suburban culture was causing. So while consumerism developed over centuries,&nbsp; the postwar consumer revolution – the one we’re&nbsp;&nbsp; all a product of and living in right now&nbsp; - was a fundamental break from the past.&nbsp;&nbsp; Mass consumption became national, international,&nbsp;&nbsp; advertising pervaded every living room through&nbsp; radio and television and then the internet.&nbsp;&nbsp; Disposable income increased (for many). Rock and&nbsp; roll, birth control, and the swinging sixties made&nbsp;&nbsp; hedonism and the enjoyment of new desires,&nbsp; respectable. But if consumerism is so broad,&nbsp;&nbsp; so vague, so far-reaching, what,&nbsp; really, does a consumer society mean? Ok, so we’ve looked at some history,&nbsp;&nbsp; what about the culture, the psychology, the&nbsp; sociology, the philosophy or consumerism. There is clearly not one single consumerism,&nbsp; though. We consume everything from food to music,&nbsp;&nbsp; to essential oils to new package experiences.&nbsp; Is it all just hedonistic? No, clearly not, we&nbsp;&nbsp; consume books and audio books and online lectures,&nbsp; we consume healthy foods we don’t necessarily&nbsp;&nbsp; enjoy, we use gadgets like sports watches&nbsp; that we don’t necessarily get pleasure from. But consumerism is even more complicated.&nbsp; It’s not just stuff. It’s a cultural activity,&nbsp;&nbsp; a subject, certain attitude towards the&nbsp; world. We have to remember that ‘going&nbsp;&nbsp; shopping’ and ‘being a consumer’ is a&nbsp; historical cultural activity that has&nbsp;&nbsp; norms – that’s done in different ways depending&nbsp; on the person, the place, the historical period.&nbsp;&nbsp; Its not just the act of purchasing and&nbsp; consumption – its the perspectives,&nbsp;&nbsp; the specific activities, the marketing,&nbsp; the relationships that go with it. Consumer groups and cooperatives&nbsp; and political parties and pressure&nbsp;&nbsp; groups and lobbies all developed&nbsp; throughout the 19the century.&nbsp;&nbsp; Consumers organized, there are book genres&nbsp; and discourses about shopping conscientiously,&nbsp;&nbsp; consumer groups lobby the states&nbsp; for rights and consumer protections. So apart from the obvious – an increase in&nbsp; global trade, the black death played a huge role,&nbsp;&nbsp; by the way – so many died that the people&nbsp; that survived were better off financially&nbsp;&nbsp; and had more disposable income – apart from this&nbsp; stuff, can we identify any cultural dynamics that&nbsp;&nbsp; might help us understand consumerism today?&nbsp; I think there are two that provide a good&nbsp;&nbsp; route into the psychology and sociology: the&nbsp; production of new desires and social mimicry. Some objects – some consumer goods,&nbsp; fulfill a simple natural biological&nbsp;&nbsp; need – we eat to satiate ourselves,&nbsp; we buy a bed to sleep on, a home&nbsp;&nbsp; to protect us from the elements. But&nbsp; we’re not just a needs species, we go&nbsp;&nbsp; beyond our simple needs, create new&nbsp; desires, new ways of being in the world. We seem to have a desire for the new&nbsp; – but really understanding what that&nbsp;&nbsp; means, has only developed over&nbsp; the past few hundred years. We can see this shift in what desire&nbsp; means across the 19th century. Sociologist Colin Campbell argues, for example&nbsp; that, Romanticism – that hugely influential&nbsp;&nbsp; pan-European movement that emphasised feelings,&nbsp; sentiments, emotions, novel experiences and&nbsp;&nbsp; adventure and creativity – was an important part&nbsp; of the development of the consumerism outlook. He wrote that the consumer ‘‘withdraw(s)&nbsp; from reality as fast as he encounters it,&nbsp;&nbsp; ever-casting his day-dreams forward in time,&nbsp; attaching them to objects of desire, and then&nbsp;&nbsp; subsequently “unhooking” them from these objects&nbsp; as and when they are attained and experienced’. As modernity developed and we were unshackled from&nbsp; the circularity of traditional life, of the same&nbsp;&nbsp; routines every day, month, and year, we began&nbsp; to want to collect something new: experiences. Oscar Wilde satirised this idea of new&nbsp; experiences in hi 1891 The Picture of Dorian Gray.&nbsp;&nbsp; Dorian exchanges his soul for everlasting youth&nbsp; so that he can live endlessly as a hedonist. As Rachel Bowlby writes that Gray ‘exchanges&nbsp; his moral self for the unbounded liberty&nbsp;&nbsp; of the new hedonist’, there is no limit to&nbsp; ‘to the number of personalities he can adopt,&nbsp;&nbsp; to the experiences he can try.’ The modern world created adventurers, explorers,&nbsp;&nbsp; dandies, and flaneurs, moving through the&nbsp; world just to collate and understand the new. Already in 1655, philosopher and scientist&nbsp; Robert Boyle had written that while&nbsp;&nbsp; ‘other creatures were content with&nbsp; easily attainable necessaries’,&nbsp;&nbsp; humans had ‘a multiplicity of&nbsp; desires’ and ‘greedy appetites.’ In 1741’s Fable of the Bees, Bernard Mandeville&nbsp; had argued against the received wisdom of&nbsp;&nbsp; Christianity, the Ancient Greeks and the Romans&nbsp; that private vice and selfish individual desire&nbsp;&nbsp; were pernicious. Instead, he said, they were&nbsp; socially useful - they created more trade,&nbsp;&nbsp; more commerce, and in the&nbsp; end made everyone better off. Scottish Enlightenment philosopher&nbsp; David Hume agreed to an extent&nbsp;&nbsp; writing that indolent luxury was still pernicious&nbsp; but that an ‘increase and consumption of all the&nbsp;&nbsp; commodities, which serve to the ornament and&nbsp; pleasures of life, are advantages to society’. When Wilde was writing in the 19th century,&nbsp; Paris was becoming an international a hotspot&nbsp;&nbsp; for new department stores placing a&nbsp; universe of exotic goods on display In The Ladies Paradise, Emile Zola wrote about&nbsp; the allure of the new stores. He called them&nbsp;&nbsp; ‘alters’ – a miracle – a ‘machine’ – and that&nbsp; ‘mad desires were driving all the women crazy. ‘ Other Parisians and critics talked about the new&nbsp; type of person: the flaneur – the person strolling&nbsp;&nbsp; aimlessly around the Parisian arcades, taking&nbsp; it all in, experiencing as much as possible. At the beginning of the 20th century, as&nbsp; Henry Ford popularized a new method of&nbsp;&nbsp; standarsized mass production, capitalists&nbsp; and then advertising executives on Madison&nbsp;&nbsp; Avenue wondered how new desires could be&nbsp; maintained, even created from thin air. The father of public relations and&nbsp; Freud’s nephew, Edward Bernays wrote that "Mass production is profitable only if its&nbsp;&nbsp; rhythm can be maintained." Business&nbsp; "cannot afford to wait until the public&nbsp;&nbsp; asks for its product; it must maintain constant&nbsp; touch, through advertising and propaganda…&nbsp;&nbsp; to assure itself the continuous demand which&nbsp; alone will make its costly plant profitable". Freud became central to the new advertising&nbsp; industry. Ad men drew on his theories of&nbsp;&nbsp; sexual desire, oral gratification, and&nbsp; the pleasure principle to sell new brands. The psychologist and marketer&nbsp; Ernest Dichter famously recommended:&nbsp;&nbsp; ‘don’t sell shoes – sell lovely feet!’ The shift was that consumerism&nbsp; and the marketing that was&nbsp;&nbsp; developing with it wasn't about needs&nbsp; it was about new fantasies, new lives,&nbsp;&nbsp; becoming a new and different person. It wasnt&nbsp; about the present, it was about the future. Lehman Brother’s Paul Mazur wrote that: "We must shift America from a&nbsp; needs, to a desires culture.&nbsp;&nbsp; People must be trained to desire, to want new&nbsp; things even before the old had been entirely&nbsp;&nbsp; consumed. We must shape a new mentality in&nbsp; America. Man's desires must overshadow his needs." Some of these new desires, new dreams and&nbsp; visions were practical – the washing machine&nbsp;&nbsp; freeing up the housewife’s time,&nbsp; the automobile, the typewriter–&nbsp;&nbsp; others were criticised as luxuries –&nbsp; sports cars and endless new fashions. Retail analyst Victor Lebow wrote in 1955:&nbsp; "Our enormously productive economy demands&nbsp;&nbsp; that we make consumption our way of life, that we&nbsp; convert the buying and use of goods into rituals,&nbsp;&nbsp; that we seek our spiritual satisfaction, our&nbsp; ego satisfaction, in consumption.… We need&nbsp;&nbsp; things consumed, burned up, replaced and&nbsp; discarded at an ever-accelerating rate." The landscape of desire was&nbsp; were production and consumption,&nbsp;&nbsp; marketing, advertising and the individual met. As the philosopher Jean Baurillard –&nbsp; who will come to shortly - points out&nbsp;&nbsp; in his classic The Consumer Society, heroes&nbsp; of production – colonizers, explorers,&nbsp;&nbsp; founders, industrialists – gave way to heroes&nbsp; of consumption – celebrities, gilded princes,&nbsp;&nbsp; and sports stars – notable figures that wore&nbsp; the latest designs and drove the flashiest cars. But we don’t just consume&nbsp; alone. We don’t invent our&nbsp;&nbsp; own desires independently. Those figures&nbsp; – the heroes of consumption – and our&nbsp;&nbsp; social life more broadly provide models for&nbsp; mimicry. We’re an inherently social species. In the old Royal Courts of Europe, adopting&nbsp; conspicuous styles stood one out from your&nbsp;&nbsp; competitors. Now, celebrities and models set&nbsp; trends, but we all like to be ahead of the pack. In 1899, the sociologist Thorstein&nbsp; Veblen coined the term ‘conspicuous&nbsp;&nbsp; consumption’ to describe the upper class&nbsp; phenomenon of buying, displaying, and&nbsp;&nbsp; wearing luxury goods to distinguish&nbsp; themselves from their peers. In 1950, the economist Harvey Leibenstein adapted&nbsp; the idea of conspicuous consumption – he noted&nbsp;&nbsp; how not only did the wealthy adopt new styles to&nbsp; set themselves apart, but that the middle classes&nbsp;&nbsp; then copied them to try and display social&nbsp; status. He called it the bandwagon effect. The Veblen effect – as its come to be known –&nbsp; means that the aquiring of consumer goods is&nbsp;&nbsp; not just about use, utility, beauty, boredom,&nbsp; or innovation – but about status. The value of&nbsp;&nbsp; the good is not determined by its usefulness&nbsp; but by the social value of displaying it. But status is not just a surface phenomenon– being&nbsp; in the know is about having the right knowledge,&nbsp;&nbsp; the right know-how, knowing the direction of the&nbsp; wind. It’s all necessary to being accepted in the&nbsp;&nbsp; correct circles, whether that’s about class,&nbsp; or being a fashion designer or an inventor.&nbsp;&nbsp; Even science has fashionable trends. As the sociologist Roberta&nbsp; Sassatelli writes there is ‘‘the knowledge (gastronomic or social)&nbsp; that derives from having eaten out,&nbsp;&nbsp; tasted this or that dish, having used a given&nbsp; type of cutlery, and having managed well the&nbsp;&nbsp; phases of the meal, that is tasting, commenting on&nbsp; the dishes, chatting and exchanging information.” This act of social mimicry&nbsp; functions in a strange way,&nbsp;&nbsp; almost as if its an engine. Often, once the vulgar&nbsp; lesser lower classes begin to adopt a style,&nbsp;&nbsp; then it no longer distinguishes the upper&nbsp; classes who brought it into fashion and used&nbsp;&nbsp; it to distinguish themselves as unique,&nbsp; as special. And so trends keep revolving&nbsp;&nbsp; as a new new trend setters turn away&nbsp; from the crowd and adopt new styles. The sociologist Georg Simmel writes that: ‘As soon&nbsp; as the lower classes begin to copy their style,&nbsp;&nbsp; thereby crossing the line of the demarcation&nbsp; the upper classes have drawn and destroying the&nbsp;&nbsp; uniformity of their coherence, the upper classes&nbsp; turn away from this style and adopt a new one,&nbsp;&nbsp; which in turn differentiated them from the&nbsp; masses; and thus the game goes merrily on.’ Baudrillard argued that in the postwar consumer&nbsp; society not only is their conspicuous consumption&nbsp;&nbsp; but a ‘fantastic conspicuousness.’ We’re&nbsp; everywhere surrounded by styles and images&nbsp;&nbsp; to imitate, new gadgets to employ, and the&nbsp; turnover rate for new fashions is inexorable. Ok, so consumerism is about creating new desires&nbsp; and social mimicry. But, you might be wondering,&nbsp;&nbsp; what's bad about this? Where’s the problem? I&nbsp; buy new camera equipment, lights, microphones,&nbsp;&nbsp; books – I have a bike that would be impossible&nbsp; to manufacture only twenty years ago.&nbsp;&nbsp; All of them are desires that have been&nbsp; created and, well, I watch reviews and&nbsp;&nbsp; see what photographers use etc, so its social&nbsp; mimicry. But I find all this stuff useful.&nbsp;&nbsp; I find the task I use them for meaningful, and&nbsp; I hope you do to. So again, where’s the problem? We can categorise the problems critics&nbsp; have identified into two parts: One is quite simple. Consumption per se isnt bad,&nbsp; but its side-effects, its unintended consequences,&nbsp;&nbsp; certain types of consumption, are. They are&nbsp; things like addiction, environmental degradation,&nbsp;&nbsp; exploitative labour practices, off-shoring, etc. I’ll return to these issues shortly. The&nbsp; other problem critics have identified is&nbsp;&nbsp; more philosophical, sociological, and&nbsp; psychological – it’s that consumerism&nbsp;&nbsp; effects us in our very psyches – we have a&nbsp; modern consumer mentality, and its out of joint. One touchstone for this type of critique&nbsp; is the economist Kenneth Galbraith’s The&nbsp;&nbsp; Affluent Society published in 1958.&nbsp; Galbraith argued that now that very&nbsp;&nbsp; basic needs of most Americans had been met,&nbsp; new demands had to be created artificially. Growth required consumption and so&nbsp; it was the job of capitalists to be&nbsp;&nbsp; endlessly creating new desires to keep people&nbsp; running circuitously around the hamster wheel. He argued that our postwar society was one&nbsp; addicted to consumption at the expense of&nbsp;&nbsp; social and political problem. In a consumer&nbsp; society, dominated by industry, growth is the&nbsp;&nbsp; central goal. There is, he says, an ‘inherently&nbsp; unstable process of consumer debt creation’,&nbsp;&nbsp; as people borrowed more and more on credit cards.&nbsp; The focus on private goods produced ‘private&nbsp;&nbsp; opulence and public squalor’ as people became&nbsp; more hedonistic and neglected their communities,&nbsp;&nbsp; civic society, and environments. He described&nbsp; people driving their ‘mauve and cerise,&nbsp;&nbsp; airconditioned, power-steered, and power-braked&nbsp; automobile’ through badly paved streets to have&nbsp;&nbsp; picnic of ‘exquisitely packaged food from&nbsp; a portable icebox by a polluted stream.’ Because we become so focused on consumption,&nbsp; other values become marginalised. We can understand this as a critique in&nbsp; the tradition of what’s often been called&nbsp;&nbsp; alienation. Karl Marx had argued that&nbsp; capitalism alienates us from our true&nbsp;&nbsp; species being. We fetishise commodities because of&nbsp; their monetary exchange value, which covers up the&nbsp;&nbsp; social processes that went into the manufacture&nbsp; of the product. We see the world through the lens&nbsp;&nbsp; of a cash nexus which covers up what’s&nbsp; really going on under the surface. Arguments about and definitions of alienation&nbsp; have taken many forms, but its most accessible&nbsp;&nbsp; popular understanding is that&nbsp; we buy stuff we don’t need,&nbsp;&nbsp; sold by adverts we don’t want to see, that&nbsp; make us desirous of things we need to desire,&nbsp;&nbsp; in an addictive society that distorts&nbsp; and discounts our true needs. In his book Empire of Things, historian Frank&nbsp; Trentmann argues that we used to see ourselves&nbsp;&nbsp; as part of the world – more holistically - and&nbsp; that the entire world and our experience of it&nbsp;&nbsp; was thought of more organically, more&nbsp; immediately, more sacredly, even. He says though that this was ‘torn asunder&nbsp; by René Descartes, who, in the 1640s,&nbsp;&nbsp; argued that the mind was an entity separate from&nbsp; the body and the material world. A century and&nbsp;&nbsp; a half later, Immanuel Kant is said to have&nbsp; completed ‘humankind’s victory over things’. What this means is that many philosophers&nbsp; of the Enlightenment argued that we could&nbsp;&nbsp; stand apart from the world, rationalise it,&nbsp; calculate it logically, see things objectively. This shift contributed to our mistaken belief&nbsp; that we could simply control the world around us,&nbsp;&nbsp; that we could manipulate and collect and examine&nbsp; objects and, as a consequence, consumer goods. Of course, we know this is a mistake. Things&nbsp; have an effect on us, the world around us has&nbsp;&nbsp; an effect on us, no matter how much we try to be&nbsp; the owner of our stuff, our stuff, in many ways,&nbsp;&nbsp; become the owners of us – they determine what we&nbsp; think, how we perceive ourselves, what we do. They&nbsp;&nbsp; can turn us into addicts, they can frustrate&nbsp; us, they can lead us down routes that aren’t&nbsp;&nbsp; good for us without us even knowing it. That’s&nbsp; the alienation argument. We’re a sick society. Marx called religion an opiate of the masses.&nbsp; It kept people docile, easily controlled,&nbsp;&nbsp; under the thumb of their rulers, distracted&nbsp; from the real problems around them. Now,&nbsp;&nbsp; many have argued, consumerism has&nbsp; become the opiate of the masses. The Enlish novelist J.B Presitly bemoaned&nbsp; in the middle of the 20th century that&nbsp;&nbsp; Britain was becoming like ‘Southern&nbsp; California, with its TV and film studios,&nbsp;&nbsp; automobile way of life (you can eat&nbsp; and drink, watch films, make love,&nbsp;&nbsp; without ever getting out of your car), its&nbsp; flavourless cosmopolitanism .and bogus religions.’ Or take this quote from Frankmann’s Empire&nbsp; of Things on a cinema goer from the 1920s: ‘One young British woman, a shorthand typist&nbsp; who went to the pictures four times a week,&nbsp;&nbsp; found that seeing ‘marvellous places’ like&nbsp; New York and California on the screen left her&nbsp;&nbsp; ‘miserable and unhappy sitting in my stuffy little&nbsp; office all day with nobody to talk to but myself&nbsp;&nbsp; (which I don’t) and to go home to a house that&nbsp; should have been knocked down five years ago.’ These quotes suggest that what we’re surrounded&nbsp; by is somehow wrong. That we’re subjected to&nbsp;&nbsp; and forced to absorb and embody great images of&nbsp; desires that we don’t have but others – mysterious&nbsp;&nbsp; others, often out of reach, often manipulated&nbsp; and polished to sell more adverts –&nbsp;&nbsp; do. The images aren’t real, they’re not&nbsp; representative of reality, they have no depth. The postmodern consumer society&nbsp; is associated with the MTV viewer,&nbsp;&nbsp; channel hopping, flipping through images with&nbsp; an easily accessible remote, listening to&nbsp;&nbsp; disjointed sounds and flashing images&nbsp; without really feeling anything. The viewer, in sociologist&nbsp; Mike Featherstone's words,&nbsp;&nbsp; ‘merely enjoys the multiphrenic intensities&nbsp; and sensations of the surface of the images.’ It’s all connected. Because flipping through MTV&nbsp; works through the same logic as fast-fashion and&nbsp;&nbsp; gadgets with built in obsolescence:&nbsp; its all fleeting, nothing lasts,&nbsp;&nbsp; quality diminishes – it’s designed so that you&nbsp; consume it quickly and move on to the next thing. This is a common critique of a postmodern&nbsp; culture that the some say underpins our consume&nbsp;&nbsp; culture. If you look at art, literature,&nbsp; and film they jump around stylistically. Fast-fashion trends come and go, styles in fashion&nbsp; and film are just stuck together in pastiche,&nbsp;&nbsp; the world moves so fast that nothing&nbsp; can be properly understood, analysed,&nbsp;&nbsp; critiqued – there is, in the philosopher Fredric&nbsp; Jameson's’ words, a depthlessness to our world. It’s driven by production and consumption but&nbsp; psychologises us by infecting our culture too. The Frankfurt School philosophers Adorno and&nbsp; Horkheimer argued that Hollywood and the wider&nbsp;&nbsp; culture industry that developed across the&nbsp; 20th century was shallow – everything was&nbsp;&nbsp; standardised to reduce costs and commodified&nbsp; to reach the widest consumer market.&nbsp;&nbsp; All culture – music, fashion, art, film –&nbsp; was reduced to the lowest common denominator&nbsp;&nbsp; so that it could be consumed by&nbsp; the largest number of consumers. They write, ‘culture today is infecting everything&nbsp; with sameness. Film, radio, and magazines forms&nbsp;&nbsp; a system. Each branch of culture is unanimous&nbsp; within itself and all are unanimous together. Each character can pop up&nbsp; interchangeably in each film.&nbsp;&nbsp; Ironman and Captain America can be&nbsp; replaced with new incarnations. Each new&nbsp;&nbsp; storyline in a television is easily recognisable.&nbsp; The same types of humour resonate throughout. And&nbsp;&nbsp; product placement is everywhere. It is simple. Easy. Consumable.&nbsp; We can see similar arguments in three more&nbsp; thinkers: Fredrich Jameson, Jean Baudrillard,&nbsp;&nbsp; and David Harvey. They all put emphasis on the&nbsp; culture of our postmodern consumer society too.&nbsp;&nbsp; That its obsessed with image, with&nbsp; shallowness and lack of depth. I want to quickly go through each one, because&nbsp; there are similarities in them that can help us&nbsp;&nbsp; understand what unifies many of the critiques&nbsp; and so we can then etter think about solutions.&nbsp;&nbsp; We’ll then turn to what they might&nbsp; have left out, what criticisms of&nbsp;&nbsp; them are, before trying to think&nbsp; about consumerism in a new way. Jean Baudrillard published the&nbsp; Consumer Society in 1970. I love&nbsp;&nbsp; this quote that really encapsulates the FOMO&nbsp; of consumerism, how forward looking he was.&nbsp;&nbsp; He wrote that consumerism came with a ‘revival&nbsp; of a universal curiosity in respect of cookery,&nbsp;&nbsp; culture, science, religion, sexuality, etc. 'Try&nbsp; Jesus!' runs an American slogan. You have to try&nbsp;&nbsp; everything, for consumerist man is haunted by&nbsp; the fear of 'missing' something, some form of&nbsp;&nbsp; enjoyment or other. You never know whether a&nbsp; particular encounter, a particular experience&nbsp;&nbsp; (Christmas in the Canaries, eel in whisky,&nbsp; the Prado, LSD, Japanese-style love-making)&nbsp;&nbsp; will not elicit some 'sensation'. It is no&nbsp; longer desire, or even 'taste', or a specific&nbsp;&nbsp; inclination that are at stake, but a generalized&nbsp; curiosity, driven by a vague sense of unease.’ Baudrillard was concerned primarily with image&nbsp; and language – the signs of consumer advertising&nbsp;&nbsp; and commercial language. He talked about&nbsp; the dizzying abundance of those signs,&nbsp;&nbsp; and argued that we were so saturated&nbsp; in the language of consumerism&nbsp;&nbsp; that that language dominated our lives more than&nbsp; any other way of communicating with each other. He wrote: ‘There is all around us today a kind&nbsp; of fantastic conspicuousness of consumption and&nbsp;&nbsp; abundance, constituted by the multiplication&nbsp; of objects, services and material goods,&nbsp;&nbsp; and this represents something of a fundamental&nbsp; mutation in the ecology of the human species.’ For Marx, under capitalism, what he called the&nbsp; ‘use value’ of an object – the real value of what&nbsp;&nbsp; its worth to me and us societally to use this mug,&nbsp; say – had been been replaced in our heads with&nbsp;&nbsp; an exchange value – we think instead about how&nbsp; much an item is worth economically. For example,&nbsp;&nbsp; someone might like this mug because it cost a lot&nbsp; of money and has diamonds implanted in the rim,&nbsp;&nbsp; not because they like it aesthetically&nbsp; or think its the perfect size.&nbsp;&nbsp; Fetishising commodities in this way also&nbsp; hides the social labour, the real work,&nbsp;&nbsp; the real lives, that went into making the&nbsp; object. We simply think of it market terms. On top of use value and exchange value,&nbsp;&nbsp; Baudrillard added a third value: sign value.&nbsp; We value things not by how useful they are,&nbsp;&nbsp; or even how much we can get for them, but&nbsp; on what they signify to others socially.&nbsp;&nbsp; I might like this mug, for example,&nbsp; because it’s a fashionable Ted Baker mug. Baudrillard argued that this sign value&nbsp; was like a hidden language we all speak. We live in a network of brand names,&nbsp; consumer images, movie stars with new shoes,&nbsp;&nbsp; and social status that depends largely&nbsp; on where we live, what we own, what our&nbsp;&nbsp; position is at work – and all of these things have&nbsp; a direct effect on what we value, what we desire,&nbsp;&nbsp; and what these things say about us to others.&nbsp; Baudrillard called this network a code,&nbsp;&nbsp; and he said we were more concerned with the&nbsp; code than we were with reality – the real He sees this consumer code as being like a&nbsp; language that speaks to us. We don’t desire&nbsp;&nbsp; new things at random – desires must be created,&nbsp; coded, they must fit into the system of fantasy.&nbsp;&nbsp; New goods must somehow be inserted&nbsp; into the existing order of consumption. Our desires aren’t just&nbsp; biological, personal, individual,&nbsp;&nbsp; or based on use-value – usefulness – or exchange&nbsp; value – that this is a gold plated laptop so&nbsp;&nbsp; will be worth more – but are&nbsp; socially constructed. We are coded&nbsp;&nbsp; to desire socially depending on others,&nbsp; and this determines how we think. Our needs aren’t rational, nor are they&nbsp; even about maximising utility. Social power&nbsp;&nbsp; rules. We are socialized, culturalised,&nbsp; consumerised by an abundance of&nbsp;&nbsp; messages, flashing lights, screens and magazines. What drives this? Baudrillard returns in many ways&nbsp; to Veblen’s theory of conspicuous consumption.&nbsp;&nbsp; We want to distinguish ourselves,&nbsp; setting ourselves apart socially. He calls this a ‘dialectic of&nbsp; conformism and originality’.&nbsp;&nbsp; We both want to conform – be part of the pack&nbsp; – and be original – stand outside the pack. There is, Baudrillard says,&nbsp; ‘a 'need of the individual to&nbsp;&nbsp; differentiate himself' as one more element&nbsp; in the repertoire of individual needs,&nbsp;&nbsp; which it sees as alternating with&nbsp; the opposite need to conform.’ Manufacturers want to fit these new desires – this&nbsp;&nbsp; code – into our lives without continuously&nbsp; upending their whole manufacturing lines. Modern furniture is designed to be flexible,&nbsp;&nbsp; mobile, interchangeable, without depth so it&nbsp; can fit in different spaces and be moved easily.&nbsp;&nbsp; Everything is in neutral colours and like&nbsp; IKEA, fits together like a puzzle, like a code. Tradition, uniqueness, odd&nbsp; shapes, personal history&nbsp;&nbsp; is no-longer important in the home. It’s about&nbsp; flatness, neutralness, every home looks the same. Apple resembles this, resembles IKEA. You’re&nbsp; sold a new Mac and now you need an iPad, iPhone,&nbsp;&nbsp; iWatch, airpods. And, if you don’t have apple,&nbsp;&nbsp; you need one because you might be left out,&nbsp; you can’t use iChat, you can’t send photos&nbsp;&nbsp; easily to your friends – the consumer code&nbsp; draws you in – if not, you’re ostracised. Baudrillard says, anticipating Apple,&nbsp;&nbsp; ‘Few objects today are offered alone,&nbsp; without a context of objects which&nbsp;&nbsp; 'speaks' them.’ - shop-windows, adverts, brand&nbsp; names, all come together in a cohesive vision.’ Baudrillard is famously pessimistic about&nbsp; this state of affairs. He argues we’re&nbsp;&nbsp; so soaked in this culture that we can no&nbsp; longer discover our true authentic needs. Everything is alienated because everything can&nbsp; be exchanged away. Nothing is transcendental.&nbsp;&nbsp; Everything is pastiche – the car advert relies&nbsp; on the sunglasses, the sunglasses are sold with&nbsp;&nbsp; holiday packages, the dream is a holiday in&nbsp; bermuda because that’s where Brad Pitt went –&nbsp;&nbsp; all of these phenomena build, construct,&nbsp; and direct your desires – but true meaning,&nbsp;&nbsp; what we really need, is lost. He uses the example of Kitsch&nbsp; products to illustrate what we means. Kitsch goods are everywhere. They’re 'trashy&nbsp; objects', 'folksy knick-knacks', 'souvenirs',&nbsp;&nbsp; he says. But they’re shallow imitations.&nbsp; They’re no longer connected to the real reason,&nbsp;&nbsp; the original reason, the piece of art or&nbsp; historical building they imitate was produced.&nbsp;&nbsp; Kitsche is the ultimate in artificial desire. HE writes: ‘Kitsch can be anywhere: in&nbsp; the detail of an object or in the plan&nbsp;&nbsp; of a new residential area, in an artificial flower&nbsp; or in a photo-novel. It can best be defined as&nbsp;&nbsp; a pseudo-object or, in other words,&nbsp; as a simulation, a copy, an imitation,&nbsp;&nbsp; a stereotype, as a dearth of real&nbsp; signification and a superabundance of signs.’ They are just ‘part of the package,&nbsp; the constellation of accessories by&nbsp;&nbsp; which the 'socio-cultural' standing&nbsp; of the average citizen is determined.’ Pop art functions in a similar way. Its all&nbsp; surface, it uses the signifiers of the world&nbsp;&nbsp; around us, and slaps it together, ignoring&nbsp; the true deep meaning that’s been hidden. He says: ‘Whereas all art up to pop was based&nbsp; on a 'depth' vision of the world, pop regards&nbsp;&nbsp; itself as homogeneous with this immanent order&nbsp; of signs: homogeneous with their industrial,&nbsp;&nbsp; mass production and hence with the artificial,&nbsp; manufactured character of the whole environment.’ WE see a similar same critique of the shallowness&nbsp; of our postmodern landscape in the American&nbsp;&nbsp; philosopher Fredric Jameson. His essay, turned&nbsp; into a book, ‘Postmodernism, or the Cultural Logic&nbsp;&nbsp; of Late Capitalism’, published in 1984, has been&nbsp; called the most discussed article of the 1980s. For Jameson, there is a particular&nbsp; reason consumer culture is shallow. He writes that our postmodern culture&nbsp; is schizophrenic, it jumps from place&nbsp;&nbsp; to place – and relies on ‘a new depthlessness,&nbsp; which finds its prolongation both in contemporary&nbsp;&nbsp; “theory” and in a whole new culture of the&nbsp; image’ and the ‘weakening of historicity,&nbsp;&nbsp; both in our relationship to public History and&nbsp; in the new forms of our private temporality.’ He argues culture has become so&nbsp; shallow that our relationship&nbsp;&nbsp; to the depth of history has been lost. Culture is fragmented because capitalism&nbsp; is fragmented. We are detached from&nbsp;&nbsp; everything because we have to be – styles&nbsp; change, trends change, conditions change,&nbsp;&nbsp; jobs change, information changes quicker&nbsp; than we can keep up, and so we’re&nbsp;&nbsp; psychologically schizophrenic,&nbsp; frantically trying to keep up. Jameson famously compares Van Gogh’s a&nbsp; pair of boots from 1887 – a modernist&nbsp;&nbsp; painting – with Andy Worholls&nbsp; postmodern 1980 Diamond Dust Shoes. He argues that Van Gogh’s shoes&nbsp; are connected to a real world. He says ‘‘the whole missing object world which&nbsp; was once [the shoes’] lived context. . .the heavy&nbsp;&nbsp; tread of the peasant woman, the loneliness&nbsp; of the field path, the hut in the clearing,&nbsp;&nbsp; the worn and broken instruments of&nbsp; labor in the furrows and at the hearth’ ON the other hand ‘Andy Warhol’s Diamond Dust&nbsp; Shoes evidently no longer speaks to us with any&nbsp;&nbsp; of the immediacy of Van Gogh’s footgear; indeed,&nbsp; I am tempted to say that it does not really speak&nbsp;&nbsp; to us at all. Nothing in this painting organizes&nbsp; even a minimal place for the viewer. . .We are&nbsp;&nbsp; witnessing the emergence of a new kind of flatness&nbsp; or depthlessness, a new kind of superficiality.’ He says the same process is visible in our broad&nbsp; cultural understanding of the Vietnam war. He says&nbsp;&nbsp; its shaped not by reality, not by real history&nbsp; or understanding, but by Hollywood films, protest&nbsp;&nbsp; music, flashy televisions news. Like Warhol’s&nbsp; shoes, its disconnected, more imaginary than real. The Marxist thinker David Harvey writes more about&nbsp; the economic foundations of this depthlessness. If you study at the supply chains of any fast&nbsp; fashion company, any tech company, in fact,&nbsp;&nbsp; the majority of consumer goods, you’ll find&nbsp; that its fractured, outsourced, subcontracted,&nbsp;&nbsp; divided into small parts and&nbsp; separate companies across the globe,&nbsp;&nbsp; a button made here, a screen there, a&nbsp; screw in one place and a thread in another. For Harvey and Jameson, these facts of&nbsp; material production – how the economy&nbsp;&nbsp; is structured - is the reason the culture that&nbsp; results from it is so shallow. It doesn’t have&nbsp;&nbsp; time to be anything but and its constantly pushing&nbsp; for new trends. By the time a story, news item,&nbsp;&nbsp; a television show, a piece of art, or even a topic&nbsp; of discussion is produced, the world is moving on,&nbsp;&nbsp; manufacturing new product lines&nbsp; in new places based on new ideas. Harvey sees our postmodern consumer&nbsp; society as a result of the shift from&nbsp;&nbsp; Fordism – vertically integrated factories&nbsp; all owned and controlled by a single company&nbsp;&nbsp; overseeing each part of the process - to&nbsp; what he calls ‘flexible accumulation.’ Flexible accumulation is defined by outsourcing&nbsp; and subcontracting to break up the process&nbsp;&nbsp; into smaller bits, offsetting risks, making&nbsp; smaller companies to compete with one another&nbsp;&nbsp; for contracts so as to get the lowest price,&nbsp; being able to drop a manufacturer and hire a&nbsp;&nbsp; new one as soon as needed, when a style or trend&nbsp; changes. This leads a fractured economic system&nbsp;&nbsp; across the globe and a fractured culture as&nbsp; a result. New communication technologies,&nbsp;&nbsp; GPS, data centres, electronic banking, etc add&nbsp; to the speed and flexibility of the system. Harvey writes that ‘the mobilization of fashion&nbsp; in mass (as opposed to elite) markets provided&nbsp;&nbsp; a means to accelerate the pace of consumption&nbsp; not only in clothing, ornament, and decoration&nbsp;&nbsp; but also across a wide swathe of life-styles&nbsp; and recreational activities (leisure and&nbsp;&nbsp; sporting habits, pop music styles, video&nbsp; and children's games, and the like).’ And so lifestyles are adopted&nbsp; and discarded at the same rate as&nbsp;&nbsp; new trends, nothing is around long&nbsp; enough to be examined, understood,&nbsp;&nbsp; or identified with in any depth – we&nbsp; become detached, postmodern, lost. Fashion trends are adopted, mimicked, stolen, and&nbsp; ripped off; fads and countercultures are co-opted,&nbsp;&nbsp; profited from, then discarded. ‘All that&nbsp; is solid melts into air,’ as Marx wrote. Everything is disposable,&nbsp; single use - napkins, cups,&nbsp;&nbsp; cameras, batteries, and pens&nbsp; are used up and thrown away. Because everything is fast a quick turnover in&nbsp; images follows. Sponsorship, television styles,&nbsp;&nbsp; new cars, new styles in cinema and&nbsp; literature, new memes and new celebrities. Harvey illustrates how this shift between&nbsp; integrated Fordist factories and global&nbsp;&nbsp; flexible accumulation has effected our&nbsp; culture with the proliferation of galleries.&nbsp;&nbsp; He says that in New York in 1945 there&nbsp; were just a handful of gallioers. Now,&nbsp;&nbsp; 150,000 artist in New York claim&nbsp; professioanl status. There are&nbsp;&nbsp; 680 galleries producing more than 15&nbsp; million art works across a decade. They’re all bound up in the production of new&nbsp; images that can be exchanged and co-opted by our&nbsp;&nbsp; consumer culture which ultimately is about&nbsp; moving things on as quickly as possible. The problem, he says, is ‘Capital flight,&nbsp;&nbsp; de industrialization of some regions,&nbsp; and the industrialization of others,&nbsp;&nbsp; the destruction of traditional working-class&nbsp; communities as power bases in class struggle.’ Ok, lets summarise. There are two critiques.&nbsp; The one we just covered is about alienation,&nbsp;&nbsp; about us psychologically. That there’s&nbsp; a depthless, everything is surface,&nbsp;&nbsp; everything moves too quickly, and, according&nbsp; to many, we’re alienated from our true needs,&nbsp;&nbsp; from the true use value of objects and goods. This&nbsp; is a kind humanist critique. It focuses on us. The other critique is somewhat simpler. It’s&nbsp; that consumerism is destroying the planet,&nbsp;&nbsp; the environment. That consumerism is&nbsp; bad not per se, but because of appalling&nbsp;&nbsp; work conditions and labour rights and&nbsp; child labour in places like Bangladesh. I want to argue now that both can be seen as a&nbsp;&nbsp; type of depthlessness – and asking&nbsp; the question ‘what’s the opposite&nbsp;&nbsp; of depthlessness?’ - what would depth mean?&nbsp; Might help point us in the right direction. This humanist problem – the problem, essentially,&nbsp; of alienation – has been criticised by people&nbsp;&nbsp; who say alienation from what? Is there really&nbsp; any true authentic self to be alienated from?&nbsp;&nbsp; Aren’t we all the product of self-creation,&nbsp;&nbsp; an assemblage of experiences, differing&nbsp; from person to person, place to place. This is Douglas Kellner’s critique&nbsp; of Baudrillard – that he says we’re&nbsp;&nbsp; surrounded by image and sign and a&nbsp; hyperreality that alienates from the&nbsp;&nbsp; real of existence, but he posits no way out,&nbsp; no theory of change. If consumerism alienates&nbsp;&nbsp; but there is no true self anyway, on&nbsp; what basis can consumerism be wrong? I think this is true, but it doesn’t mean&nbsp; we can’t be alienated from our true needs,&nbsp;&nbsp; that certain things have a negative effect on us&nbsp; and the world, and other things a better effect.&nbsp;&nbsp; That some states of affairs in the&nbsp; world are more desirable than others. I want to quickly draw from two philosophers&nbsp; – Sartre & Spinoza - to make the case that to&nbsp;&nbsp; challenge the negatives of consumerism,&nbsp; we should aim to turn the depthlessness&nbsp;&nbsp; that is central to critiques&nbsp; of it into its opposite: depth.&nbsp;&nbsp; In turn, we aim to turn an alienated&nbsp; life into a more fulfilling one. The anthropologist Mary Douglas&nbsp; wrote that the ‘main problem of&nbsp;&nbsp; social life is to pin down meanings so&nbsp; that they stay still for a little time.’ Whether its fast moving images or our clothes&nbsp; being made by children thousands of miles away,&nbsp;&nbsp; this idea holds. There’s so much going on&nbsp; that it’s impossible to understand it all,&nbsp;&nbsp; to pin it down – and so in many areas we live&nbsp; shallowly, not understanding, not thinking,&nbsp;&nbsp; not able to fully comprehend what goes into&nbsp; the construction of our every day lives. So how do we pin down our fast moving&nbsp; consumer culture to analyse it in depth? Spinoza suggested that we look at&nbsp; the world from what we described as&nbsp;&nbsp; the perspective of eternity. We should examine&nbsp; the causes of things – where they come from,&nbsp;&nbsp; how they function, we should always analyse our&nbsp; ideas, actions, and desires, looking outwards&nbsp;&nbsp; and understanding them in the wider context of&nbsp; the world. We should look at things, essentially,&nbsp;&nbsp; in depth. We should understand where our food and&nbsp; clothes come from and the effect their having. And the French existentialist Jean-Paul Sartre&nbsp; made a similar point. He argued that our projects,&nbsp;&nbsp; our ideas, our opinions, and values – our&nbsp; perspective on the world – should rhyme. We should&nbsp;&nbsp; look at how things inform and effect one another. He argues that our experiences should together in&nbsp;&nbsp; a structure of meaning that we then impose&nbsp; onto the world. Meaning can be made deeper,&nbsp;&nbsp; more connected, more, well, meaningful, by&nbsp; uncovering and creating patterns between&nbsp;&nbsp; things so that the world rhymes, so&nbsp; that there’s rhythm and coherence.&nbsp; So if I make Youtube videos, I&nbsp; become interested in cameras to film,&nbsp;&nbsp; but I also then become interested in photography,&nbsp; and in how and where my cameras are made,&nbsp;&nbsp; what other things I can do with them,&nbsp; the history and philosophy of them.&nbsp; These are brief references to two philosophers&nbsp; I’ve introduced in depth before on the channel,&nbsp;&nbsp; but I think they get at something that shines a&nbsp; light on the depthlessness of consumer culture.&nbsp;&nbsp; And they help with a problem I think thinkers&nbsp; like Jameson and Baudrillard run into.&nbsp; After reading them you have the&nbsp; impression that consumerism is one&nbsp;&nbsp; totalising thing thing, everything gets lumped&nbsp; in with it – everything is just consumed and all&nbsp;&nbsp; images and adverts and consumer goods are equally&nbsp; negative and equally alienating. It ignores that&nbsp;&nbsp; not all things are consumed ‘shallowly’, some are&nbsp; experienced, approached, and consumed with depth. I buy new cameras and microphones,&nbsp; books, a drone, clothes, new foods,&nbsp;&nbsp; seek new recipes and holidays – precisely because&nbsp; they bring me the opposite of depthlessness,&nbsp;&nbsp; they create depth in my life. So to finish,&nbsp; lets have look at how depth is created, but&nbsp;&nbsp; what the limits of this way of&nbsp; thinking in this way might be. Sassatelli argues that often, rather than&nbsp; simply consuming brainlessly, consumers are&nbsp;&nbsp; active in the act of consumption. They’re&nbsp; also often decodifying, reinterpreting, and&nbsp;&nbsp; criticizing the PR, adverts, and products&nbsp; they consume. We’re not just zombies,&nbsp;&nbsp; taken in by every advertising board we see. She writes that ‘consumption often consists&nbsp; in re-framing the meaning and uses of material&nbsp;&nbsp; culture by translating the purely commercial value&nbsp; of goods into other forms of value: affection,&nbsp;&nbsp; relationships, symbolism, status, normality, etc,’ She continues ‘recent studies of audiences and&nbsp; media consumption show that spectators actively&nbsp;&nbsp; de-codify messages and images, referring them&nbsp; to the context in which they are experienced,&nbsp;&nbsp; to their own social position,&nbsp; gender, life course, and so on.’ Take DIY. Yes, new tools and paints and furniture&nbsp; are advertised and sold to us, but they’re often&nbsp;&nbsp; decommodified, made personal, adapted, decorated&nbsp; and adorned with family photos or meaningful&nbsp;&nbsp; items. There is a ‘de-commoditized sphere’ in the&nbsp; home, or between friends, at events and online. We use musical instruments to create&nbsp; new, individual, unique music, we&nbsp;&nbsp; paint and discuss and trade on social&nbsp; media, we fill photo books, we give&nbsp;&nbsp; gifts – all of these things transcend the flatness&nbsp; of consumerism and bring in more personal, social,&nbsp;&nbsp; ethical, or transcendental ideals. In 1970, Baudrillard pointed to the typewriter&nbsp; that can write in 13 different fonts and a&nbsp;&nbsp; diction machine to record notes as examples of&nbsp; pointless gadgets. To me, these seem useful – I&nbsp;&nbsp; use different fonts to convey different ideas&nbsp; or emotional tone quickly – its another layer to&nbsp;&nbsp; language. And others find recording to remember&nbsp; important things easier than jotting them down. Sassatelli also points to how&nbsp; the Walkman was criticized when&nbsp;&nbsp; it was first released for representing&nbsp; ‘aleination, anomie, and atomization’&nbsp;&nbsp; despite listening to music on the go clearly&nbsp; also being representative of many other&nbsp;&nbsp; things – escape, heightened experience,&nbsp; resistance, creativity, energy. She also writes about the positive experience&nbsp; of connoissuership, of collectibles, of hobbies: ‘the pervasive allure of connoisseurship may be&nbsp; seen as having to do with mastering objects and&nbsp;&nbsp; demonstrating aestheticized detachment from&nbsp; them: by piling up a complex repertoire of&nbsp;&nbsp; knowledge, the connoisseur finds&nbsp; a way to get close to commodities&nbsp;&nbsp; while distancing him or herself&nbsp; from their immediate hold.‘ Others have pointed to cinema, television,&nbsp; and image being deeper than is often assumed. In his book on Baudrillard, Adam Roberts&nbsp; writes that for Baudrillard Vietnam was&nbsp;&nbsp; ‘a war flattened and emptied out to a basic layer&nbsp; of violence, mixed in with popular culture and TV,&nbsp;&nbsp; accompanied in many people’s imagination&nbsp; by a soundtrack of 1960s pop-music.&nbsp;&nbsp; But the violence and death was not&nbsp; ‘hyper-real’ or ‘simulated’, it was real.’ My point is, in among the problems&nbsp; we’ve identified with consumer society,&nbsp;&nbsp; everywhere we look we can also find depth,&nbsp; meaning, usefulness, creativity, connection&nbsp;&nbsp; too. And we can apply this seeking after&nbsp; depth to our understanding of how things like&nbsp;&nbsp; supply chains work, what we think is&nbsp; unethical and ethical, what we want to support&nbsp;&nbsp; and avoid, what we want to push to regulate,&nbsp; organise against, and politically change.&nbsp;&nbsp; I think this is the opposite of&nbsp; depthlessness, the opposite of alienation. But am I being naive? Am I ignoring other&nbsp; issues? The breadth and scope of the problem.&nbsp;&nbsp; The addiction to new fashions, pointless&nbsp; new purchases, pollution or child labour? I guess, at least in part, what I’m arguing is&nbsp; that if you look at the consequences of your&nbsp;&nbsp; purchases in depth, if you think about the&nbsp; message, the result, the meaning in your own&nbsp;&nbsp; and other’s lives, then you can become&nbsp; an ethical consumer. Is this true? Even as that term ‘ethical consumption’ has&nbsp; become more popular, as we’ve become increasingly&nbsp;&nbsp; adware of the pitfalls of fast fashion and the&nbsp; environmental desegregation of consumption,&nbsp;&nbsp; Bottled water sales, to take&nbsp; just one simple example,&nbsp;&nbsp; have continued to rise and are projected&nbsp; to to so indefinitely. Palm oil – a major&nbsp;&nbsp; cause of deforestation - is still in half&nbsp; of packaged products. Fast fashion companies&nbsp;&nbsp; continue to pay below the minimum wage.&nbsp; Garment factories continue to burn down. But working out whether more conscious&nbsp; consumerism works at all is difficult to do. On the one hand, we continue to outsource to&nbsp; Bangladesh and see these tragedies that are&nbsp;&nbsp; the result of poor health and safety, terrible&nbsp; regulations, and labour practices, but at the&nbsp;&nbsp; same time, the ILO – the International Labour&nbsp; Organisation – says that child labour is down&nbsp;&nbsp; by a third since 2000. 61% of fashion companies&nbsp; have committed to using sustainable fabrics. That is, when there’s cultural&nbsp; pressure on manufacturers,&nbsp;&nbsp; on what we eat and wear there&nbsp; does appear to be some change. On the other hand, NorthWestern&nbsp; University professor Brayden King&nbsp;&nbsp; has argued boycotts have a negligible effect&nbsp; on a companies sales revenue. He says that the&nbsp;&nbsp; problem is “we can’t pay attention to&nbsp; any single controversy for very long.” Yes, we talk about the tragic Rana Plaze&nbsp; building collapse in Bangladesh in 2013 when&nbsp;&nbsp; 1134 died for a bit, but then&nbsp; we move on to the other things. Terry Hathaway at the LSE writes that ‘Even the&nbsp; most hyper-vigilant, well-informed shopper cannot&nbsp;&nbsp; know everything – the labour (and conditions),&nbsp; the tools (and the production of these tools),&nbsp;&nbsp; the raw materials (and their extraction), the&nbsp; energy, etc. – that goes into making even the most&nbsp;&nbsp; simple products. Instead, the consumer must buy in&nbsp; partial ignorance of all of the factors, primarily&nbsp;&nbsp; on the basis of those attributes that are the most&nbsp; conspicuous and, to some degree, based on trust.’ He continues ‘shopping at Whole Foods could be&nbsp; construed as support for higher welfare meat&nbsp;&nbsp; and organic agricultural methods,&nbsp; but it will equally support&nbsp;union&nbsp;&nbsp; busting,&nbsp;low wages&nbsp;for workers&nbsp; and&nbsp;right-wing libertarian politics.’ So here is the crux of it. Our consumer society&nbsp; is messy, complex; it both sustains us creatively&nbsp;&nbsp; and destroys the planet. So maybe it is a&nbsp; useless concept. I think we’re better off&nbsp;&nbsp; thinking about it in its original sense – to use&nbsp; up – as, not a noun that describes our soceity,&nbsp;&nbsp; but as a verb -as something we’re all doing. And&nbsp; like anything we do, there is a chain of activity,&nbsp;&nbsp; social and historical, environmental and economic&nbsp; – that supports the consumption and that we in&nbsp;&nbsp; turn support by consuming. Only by studying the&nbsp; understanding the issues in depth can we grapple&nbsp;&nbsp; with these problems. Thinking about regulation,&nbsp; changing the economic system, organising&nbsp;&nbsp; politically is a part of this depth – everyone’s&nbsp; interpretation of what to do will be different. For example, journalist Alden Wicker&nbsp; argues ethical consumption doesn’t work.&nbsp;&nbsp; We should instead donate some of&nbsp; the money we spend going greener&nbsp;&nbsp; on donations to NGOs and green politicians. And commentator Robert Reich has reminded&nbsp; us that when “you hear a company boast about&nbsp;&nbsp; how environmentally friendly it is, hold the&nbsp; applause. Under super-competitive capitalism—what&nbsp;&nbsp; I’ve termed ‘supercapitalism’—it’s&nbsp; naive to think corporations can or&nbsp;&nbsp; will sacrifice profits and shareholder&nbsp; returns in order to fight global warming.&nbsp;&nbsp; Firms that go green to improve their&nbsp; public relations, or cut their costs,&nbsp;&nbsp; or anticipate regulations are&nbsp; being smart, not virtuous.” So its important to approach this&nbsp; with a multifaceted attitude.&nbsp;&nbsp; Depth means more eyes on all the issues.&nbsp; Depth means engaging with the politics. Depth&nbsp;&nbsp; means exploring our own desires and beliefs&nbsp; vigilantly. Taking an issue – something we&nbsp;&nbsp; consume - and understanding the ramifications,&nbsp; something I want to try to apply next time.

- The dirty secret of capitalism -- and a new way forward | Nick Hanauer: Kind: captions Language: en I am a capitalist, and after a 30-year career in capitalism spanning three dozen companies, generating tens of billions of dollars in market value, I'm not just in the top one percent, I'm in the top .01 percent of all earners. Today, I have come to share the secrets of our success, because rich capitalists like me have never been richer. So the question is, how do we do it? How do we manage to grab an ever-increasing share of the economic pie every year? Is it that rich people are smarter than we were 30 years ago? Is it that we're working harder than we once did? Are we taller, better looking? Sadly, no. It all comes down to just one thing: economics. Because, here's the dirty secret. There was a time in which the economics profession worked in the public interest, but in the neoliberal era, today, they work only for big corporations and billionaires, and that is creating a little bit of a problem. We could choose to enact economic policies that raise taxes on the rich, regulate powerful corporations or raise wages for workers. We have done it before. But neoliberal economists would warn that all of these policies would be a terrible mistake, because raising taxes always kills economic growth, and any form of government regulation is inefficient, and raising wages always kills jobs. Well, as a consequence of that thinking, over the last 30 years, in the USA alone, the top one percent has grown 21 trillion dollars richer while the bottom 50 percent have grown 900 billion dollars poorer, a pattern of widening inequality that has largely repeated itself across the world. And yet, as middle class families struggle to get by on wages that have not budged in about 40 years, neoliberal economists continue to warn that the only reasonable response to the painful dislocations of austerity and globalization is even more austerity and globalization. So, what is a society to do? Well, it's super clear to me what we need to do. We need a new economics. So, economics has been described as the dismal science, and for good reason, because as much as it is taught today, it isn't a science at all, in spite of all of the dazzling mathematics. In fact, a growing number of academics and practitioners have concluded that neoliberal economic theory is dangerously wrong and that today's growing crises of rising inequality and growing political instability are the direct result of decades of bad economic theory. What we now know is that the economics that made me so rich isn't just wrong, it's backwards, because it turns out it isn't capital that creates economic growth, it's people; and it isn't self-interest that promotes the public good, it's reciprocity; and it isn't competition that produces our prosperity, it's cooperation. What we can now see is that an economics that is neither just nor inclusive can never sustain the high levels of social cooperation necessary to enable a modern society to thrive. So where did we go wrong? Well, it turns out that it's become painfully obvious that the fundamental assumptions that undergird neoliberal economic theory are just objectively false, and so today first I want to take you through some of those mistaken assumptions and then after describe where the science suggests prosperity actually comes from. So, neoliberal economic assumption number one is that the market is an efficient equilibrium system, which basically means that if one thing in the economy, like wages, goes up, another thing in the economy, like jobs, must go down. So for example, in Seattle, where I live, when in 2014 we passed our nation's first 15 dollar minimum wage, the neoliberals freaked out over their precious equilibrium. "If you raise the price of labor," they warned, "businesses will purchase less of it. Thousands of low-wage workers will lose their jobs. The restaurants will close." Except ... they didn't. The unemployment rate fell dramatically. The restaurant business in Seattle boomed. Why? Because there is no equilibrium. Because raising wages doesn't kill jobs, it creates them; because, for instance, when restaurant owners are suddenly required to pay restaurant workers enough so that now even they can afford to eat in restaurants, it doesn't shrink the restaurant business, it grows it, obviously. (Applause) Thank you. The second assumption is that the price of something is always equal to its value, which basically means that if you earn 50,000 dollars a year and I earn 50 million dollars a year, that's because I produce a thousand times as much value as you. Now, it will not surprise you to learn that this is a very comforting assumption if you're a CEO paying yourself 50 million dollars a year but paying your workers poverty wages. But please, take it from somebody who has run dozens of businesses: this is nonsense. People are not paid what they are worth. They are paid what they have the power to negotiate, and wages' falling share of GDP is not because workers have become less productive but because employers have become more powerful. And -- And by pretending that the giant imbalance in power between capital and labor doesn't exist, neoliberal economic theory became essentially a protection racket for the rich. The third assumption, and by far the most pernicious, is a behavioral model that describes human beings as something called "homo economicus," that we are all perfectly selfish, perfectly rational and relentlessly self-maximizing. But just ask yourselves, is it plausible that every single time for your entire life, when you did something nice for somebody else, all you were doing was maximizing your own utility? Is it plausible that when a soldier jumps on a grenade to defend fellow soldiers, they're just promoting their narrow self-interest? If you think that's nuts, contrary to any reasonable moral intuition, that's because it is and, according to the latest science, not true. But it is this behavioral model which is at the cold, cruel heart of neoliberal economics, and it is as morally corrosive as it is scientifically wrong because, if we accept at face value that humans are fundamentally selfish, and then we look around the world at all of the unambiguous prosperity in it, then it follows logically, then it must be true by definition, that billions of individual acts of selfishness magically transubstantiate into prosperity and the common good. If we humans are merely selfish maximizers, then selfishness is the cause of our prosperity. Under this economic logic, greed is good, widening inequality is efficient, and the only purpose of the corporation can be to enrich shareholders, because to do otherwise would be to slow economic growth and harm the economy overall. And it is this gospel of selfishness which forms the ideological cornerstone a way of thinking which has produced economic policies which have enabled me and my rich buddies in the top one percent to grab virtually all of the benefits of growth over the last 40 years. But, if instead we accept the latest empirical research, real science, which correctly describes human beings as highly cooperative, reciprocal and intuitively moral creatures, then it follows logically that it must be cooperation and not selfishness that is the cause of our prosperity, and it isn't our self-interest but rather our inherent reciprocity that is humanity's economic superpower. So at the heart of this new economics is a story about ourselves that grants us permission to be our best selves, and, unlike the old economics, this is a story that is virtuous and also has the virtue of being true. I want to emphasize that this new economics is not something I have personally imagined or invented. Its theories and models are being developed and refined in universities around the world building on some of the best new research in economics, complexity theory, evolutionary theory, psychology, anthropology and other disciplines. And although this new economics does not yet have its own textbook or even a commonly agreed upon name, in broad strokes its explanation of where prosperity comes from goes something like this. So, market capitalism is an evolutionary system in which prosperity emerges through a positive feedback loop between increasing amounts of innovation and increasing amounts of consumer demand. Innovation is the process by which we solve human problems, consumer demand is the mechanism through which the market selects for useful innovations, and as we solve more problems, we become more prosperous. But as we become more prosperous, our problems and solutions become more complex, and this increasing technical complexity requires ever higher levels of social and economic cooperation in order to produce the more highly specialized products that define a modern economy. Now, the old economics is correct, of course, that competition plays a crucial role in how markets work, but what it fails to see is that it is largely a competition between highly cooperative groups -- competition between firms, competition between networks of firms, competition between nations -- and anyone who has ever run a successful business knows that building a cooperative team by including the talents of everyone is almost always a better strategy than just a bunch of selfish jerks. So how do we leave neoliberalism behind and build a more sustainable, more prosperous and more equitable society? The new economics suggests just five rules of thumb. First is that successful economies are not jungles, they're gardens, which is to say that markets, like gardens, must be tended, that the market is the greatest social technology ever invented for solving human problems, but unconstrained by social norms or democratic regulation, markets inevitably create more problems than they solve. Climate change, the great financial crisis of 2008 are two easy examples. The second rule is that inclusion creates economic growth. So the neoliberal idea that inclusion is this fancy luxury to be afforded if and when we have growth is both wrong and backwards. The economy is people. Including more people in more ways is what causes economic growth in market economies. The third principle is the purpose of the corporation is not merely to enrich shareholders. The greatest grift in contemporary economic life is the neoliberal idea that the only purpose of the corporation and the only responsibility of executives is to enrich themselves and shareholders. The new economics must and can insist that the purpose of the corporation is to improve the welfare of all stakeholders: customers, workers, community and shareholders alike. Rule four: greed is not good. Being rapacious doesn't make you a capitalist, it makes you a sociopath. (Laughter) And in an economy as dependent upon cooperation at scale as ours, sociopathy is as bad for business as it is for society. And fifth and finally, unlike the laws of physics, the laws of economics are a choice. Now, neoliberal economic theory has sold itself to you as unchangeable natural law, when in fact it's social norms and constructed narratives based on pseudoscience. If we truly want a more equitable, and more sustainable economy, if we want high-functioning democracies and civil society, we must have a new economics. And here's the good news: if we want a new economics, all we have to do is choose to have it. Moderator: So Nick, I'm sure you get this question a lot. If you're so unhappy with the economic system, why not just give all your money away and join the 99 percent? Nick Hanauer: Yeah, no, yes, right. You get that a lot. You get that a lot. "If you care so much about taxes, why don't you pay more, and if you care so much about wages, why don't you pay more?" And I could do that. The problem is, it doesn't make that much difference, and I have discovered a strategy that works literally a hundred thousand times better -- Moderator: OK. NH: which is to use my money to build narratives and to pass laws that will require all the other rich people to pay taxes and pay their workers better. And so, for example, the 15-dollar minimum wage that we cooked up has now affected 30 million workers. So that works better. Moderator: That's great. If you change your mind, we'll find some takers for you. NH: OK. Thank you. Moderator: Thank you very much.
