- Does Consumerism Lead To Social Alienation In Socialist Critique? - Socialism Explained: Kind: captions Language: en [Music] Does consumerism lead to social alienation in socialist critique? Imagine a world where buying things is more than just a way to meet needs. It's a way to define who we are. That's what socialist critics often point out about consumerism. They say it can create a sense of distance between people and their true selves. When capitalism pushes us to buy more and more, it turns human relationships into market exchanges. Instead of connecting as people, we start seeing each other as commodities or sources of profit. This process is called social alienation. And it's a big concern for those rethinking how our economy works. In socialist thought, alienation happens when people feel disconnected from their work, their community, or even their own identity. Consumerism makes this worse because it encourages us to measure our worth by what we own or how much we spend. Instead of focusing on shared social goals or personal growth, we become trapped in a cycle of consumption driven by profit motives. Workers, for example, often only earn enough to keep working, not enough to enjoy meaningful leisure or personal fulfillment. Their consumption becomes about survival, not about expressing creativity or community. Another issue is how consumer culture turns human relationships into market transactions. People start to see each other as objects or exchangeable units. Social bonds weaken because interactions are filtered through the lens of market success. This process is called commodification and it leads to feelings of loneliness and disconnection. When social life becomes about buying and selling, authentic human bonds can fade away. A further point is commodity fetishism which is a fancy way of saying that we forget the social labor behind the goods we buy. We see products as independent objects of desire, not as the result of collective effort. This mystification hides the reality of exploitation and social interdependence, deepening feelings of alienation. However, some socialist thinkers see potential in consumer culture. They believe that consumption can inspire new social ideas and creative ways of living. If organized differently, consumption could support community building and social equality. The key is to shift from profit-driven consumption to practices that prioritize social needs and environmental sustainability. Reimagining economic systems involves transforming how we produce and consume. Instead of focusing on profit, future models could emphasize cooperation, shared ownership, and democratic control. This means creating workplaces where workers have a say and markets that serve human and ecological well-being. Such changes could help reduce alienation by reconnecting people to their labor, their communities, and the environment. Practically, this might look like supporting cooperatives, planning economies, or promoting local sustainable consumption. These approaches aim to make sure that goods and services are produced and used in ways that benefit everyone, not just a few. They also help people feel more connected to the process of making and sharing rather than just buying and discarding. In the end, socialist critique suggests that consumerism under capitalism fosters social alienation by turning people into commodities and hiding the social labor behind goods. To build a future where people feel more connected and fulfilled, we need to rethink how we organize production and consumption. This involves creating systems that prioritize human needs, social bonds, and ecological health over profit. Only then can we move toward an economy that truly serves us all.

- What Is Lefebvre's Critique Of Everyday Life? - History of Communism: Kind: captions Language: en What is Levverra's critique of everyday life? Have you ever thought about how your daily life is shaped by larger forces? Henry Leofever's critique of everyday life dives into this idea, especially in the context of capitalism. He believed that daily existence is not just about work or wages. Instead, it is influenced by routines, habits, and how we consume goods and ideas. Lefaber argued that everyday life has become a battleground for capitalist power. He described modern society as a bureaucratic society of controlled consumption. In this view, markets are not places of true freedom. Instead, they direct people on what to buy, how to behave, and even how to think. Leisure and creativity are often managed and limited. People are taught to find happiness and status through purchasing items. Advertising and media play a significant role in shaping our desires and lifestyles, making daily life feel scripted and artificial. From a communist perspective, Levver's analysis is vital. It shows how capitalism extends its reach into the most ordinary aspects of life, not just at work. He believed that real change requires more than just an economic revolution. It means transforming daily life itself to be more free and creative, reducing the dominance of routines and consumer pressures. This idea connects to early socialist and communist thinkers who sought to change not only who own the factories but also how people live together. Lefaber was critical of both capitalism and Soviet style communism. He envisioned true communism as expanding democratic freedoms and making life more meaningful for everyone. He argued against simply replacing one from of control with another. His work suggests that a communist future would involve rethinking how we experience daily life. It calls for breaking free from the routines and expectations set by markets and bureaucratic states. Practical applications of Lefvra's ideas can be seen in urban design, public space usage, and community organization. For communists, this means fighting for economic equality and the right to shape everyday experiences in more democratic and fulfilling ways. Lefra's work remains relevant for anyone interested in building a society where daily life is not just about survival or consumption. Instead, it should focus on creativity, connection, and real freedom.

- The Psychology Behind Every Luxury Purchase You Make: Kind: captions Language: en Luxury brands are some of the best marketers in the world because when you really think about it, why does a leather bag cost $5,000 when another costs 300? Why do people wait months, sometimes even years, for a particular watch? And why does a simple logo make someone feel wealthier or more important? The answer is that luxury brands are not just selling you a product. They're selling you access to a world that feels more exclusive and more desirable than ordinary [music] life. And that's why luxury is so powerful. A watch can tell the same time as your phone. A bag can carry the same items as one that costs a fraction of the price. A shirt can cover your body whether it has a designer label or not. But luxury brands understand that people do not buy purely based on function. >> [music] >> They buy based on emotion, aspiration, and the story they want to tell about themselves. So, in this video, we're breaking down the science behind luxury brands and exactly how they make you want to buy, the good and the bad, and what to watch out for. Let's dive into the subtle psychology that makes certain types of products feel almost impossible to resist. >> Before we start, I'm Nick and welcome to my channel where I discuss gent style and lifestyle. I make videos on a wide variety of topics on how to dress well and how to live well. So, if that interests you, then click subscribe. Also, if you love men's style, follow me on Instagram. I'm posting there regularly and I have a ton of fun content planned. [music] The first thing that luxury brands understand is that people rarely buy luxury for the product alone. They buy it for what the product represents. A watch is not just a watch. A bag is not just a bag. And a pair of shoes is not just something to walk in. >> At a certain level, these objects become symbols of a lifestyle that suggests taste and status. Someone doesn't just buy a Rolex because it tells time. They buy it because it represents achievement. [music] Someone doesn't just buy an Hermes bag because it carries their belongings. They buy it because it represents exclusivity and craftsmanship. [music] And someone doesn't just buy a Ralph Lauren blazer simply because they need a jacket. They buy into the world Ralph Lauren has created, that of country houses, polo fields, Ivy League [music] charm, and old money elegance. The product is only one part of the appeal. The real desire comes from the identity affixed to it. Luxury brands are excellent at selling you a more successful, tasteful version of yourself. And once you believe the product brings you closer to that identity, it no longer feels just like a purchase. Starts to feel like a step towards becoming someone. Luxury brands are brilliant at storytelling. And if the last section was about what luxury makes you feel about yourself, this section is about what luxury makes you believe about the brand. Luxury brands invest a great deal of time and money building a whole world around their brand. [music] They leverage themes like heritage, craftsmanship, the founders, famous clients. Perhaps even they throw in some historic moments and traditions behind the brand. For example, Louis Vuitton is selling the romance of travel. [music] Hermes is selling equestrian heritage, handcrafted old-world refinement. And Cartier is selling royalty, elegance, and timeless sophistication. This all matters because the story makes an object feel more meaningful, which allows the consumer to develop an emotional attachment to the product and to the overall brand. This is why luxury brands constantly remind you where they come from. The longer the history, the deeper the mythology. And the deeper the mythology, the easier it is for the customer to believe that they are not just buying an object, they are buying [music] a small piece of culture and tradition. Another major way luxury brands make you want to buy is through scarcity. When something is difficult to access, [music] it immediately feels more valuable. It's simple supply and demand. If anyone can walk into a store and buy it, the product may still be beautiful, but it loses some of its mystique. But when there's a waitlist because of limited production or perhaps certain pieces are reserved only for top clients, the product feels more exclusive. That exclusivity creates desire because it makes a customer feel like owning the item means they're a part of an elite club. >> You see this especially with certain watches, handbags, rare fragrances, and made-to-order pieces. A great example is the Patek Philippe Nautilus in Tiffany [music] blue. Released in 2021, only 170 were made to celebrate the long relationship between Patek Philippe and Tiffany [music] & Co. One piece was later sold for over $6 million showing [music] just how scarce and desired this watch is. In normal retail, brands try to make buying as easy as [music] possible. But in luxury, a little scarcity can turn a purchase into an achievement. Luxury brands also understand that price is not just a number, but a part of the story. In ordinary retail, a high price can scare people away. But in luxury, a high price can actually make a product feel more desirable to the right audience >> because it signals exclusivity and importance. If something costs significantly more than the average alternative, people often assume that there must be something special about it. That does not mean that price and quality are always the same thing. Sometimes the price reflects better materials and craftsmanship. Other times you're paying heavily for the name and the feeling that the brand has created around the product. >> A good example of this is the iconic Burberry trench coat. Burberry's Kensington Heritage trench is around $2,400. You [music] can find similar coats for a lot less, but here the high price point is a part of the brand's exclusivity. These companies know that if everyone can afford it, it loses a part of its status. So, the price itself becomes a filter. It tells the customer that this is not for everyone. And strangely enough, that's often what makes people want it even more. Another way luxury brands create desire is through social proof. People naturally pay attention to what highly admired and successful people are wearing. So, when a luxury product appears on a celebrity, an athlete, a royal, or a cultural icon, it becomes more than just an item. It becomes a signal. This is why brands are incredibly careful about who they associate with. The right person can make a product blow up. For example, James Bond, as we all know, wears an Omega. Steve McQueen wore a TAG Heuer Monaco. Or think about Princess Diana carrying the Lady Dior. These associations all give those products a story beyond the object itself. I have a bottle of Macallan whiskey just behind me simply because it's the whiskey of choice for James Bond. [music] So, association works. Once enough admired people are seen wearing or using something, the desire [music] spreads. Not because everyone has it, but because the right people do. Luxury brands go beyond the product itself and carefully design the entire buying experience around how they want you to feel. A luxury boutique is rarely designed to feel like an ordinary store. The lighting is softer and the displays are more selective. The space is quieter, it's more polished, and it's often more intimidating. They may also have private appointments, coffee or champagne available, careful packaging, and sales associates who make the experience feel personal. All of this creates theater around the purchase. You're not simply walking in, grabbing something off the shelf, and then leaving. You're stepping into the world of the brand. >> The boutique becomes a part of the exclusive private club that the brand is trying to curate. And the more elevated the experience feels, the easier it is to believe that the product is more valuable. That's also why luxury packaging matters, too. Everything from the box to the ribbon. [music] It's all designed to make the purchase feel ceremonial. Luxury brands understand that when the buying experience feels special, the product feels special, too. The final thing that luxury brands do is they understand that the most powerful purchases are often emotional. A good number of people rarely buy luxury items simply because they need something. They buy it to mark a moment, whether it's a promotion, a milestone birthday, a wedding, a graduation, an anniversary, or a personal achievement. The product becomes connected to a memory, which makes it feel more meaningful than an ordinary purchase. This is why luxury brands often invoke words like timeless, [music] iconic, heirloom, legacy, and investment piece. They want the product to feel like something that belongs to your life story, like a watch that you buy after building your career or a suit that you wear on an important day. That emotional connection is powerful because it makes the product feel deeply personal. Of course, there's another darker side to luxury. Luxury can represent quality products, but it can also tap into a person's insecurity and fear of being left out. This is where desire becomes manipulation. There's a difference between buying a watch because you love its design and engineering and buying it because you've been made to feel inadequate without it. It's always important to remember that a famous logo does not automatically make something stylish [music] and a high price point elegant. And owning luxury does not automatically make someone refined. This is why you have to be honest about why you want something. Are you buying it because it genuinely fits into your life and your taste? Or are you buying it because you hope it will change how people see you? A gentleman can appreciate luxury, but he must never be controlled by it. So, the answer is to buy carefully and with taste. Before buying something expensive, ask yourself a simple question. Would I still want this if no one knew the brand? If the answer is no, you may be buying it more for the name rather than the object itself. Look for pieces that genuinely suit your personal style, your lifestyle, and your wardrobe. Luxury should feel like an extension of your taste, not a costume that you put on to impress people. Then look for heritage. Some luxury pieces are desirable because they come from real tradition, like the Cartier Tank. This is actually something that's on my list to buy. Then there's also the Burberry trench, a Barbour jacket, an Hermes tie, or a pair of Edward Green shoes. The history gives them more meaning than just the logo alone. >> And most importantly, look for quality. Pay attention to the materials, the construction, the fit, the finishing, and the longevity. [music] Ask yourself, will this age well? Can it be repaired? Does it feel beautiful in person? Real luxury isn't about being flashy or flexing what you bought to others. It's about taste, craftsmanship, and buying items with a deeper meaning. There's nothing wrong with appreciating beautiful things. Just make sure you know what's being sold to you. would still consider expensive things to be better and more effective even when they are no different from their cheaper counterparts according to a study conducted by scientists at the california institute of technology and stanford united there are changes to the human brain when they discover they are making use of expensive items the study showed that people believe that the taste of wine is better when they're told it is expensive an mri scan of the brain revealed that they do enjoy it more when they think that the wine is indeed more expensive similar results were observed in another study when a placebo was used in place of an analgesic the participants who took the pill that were supposedly worth two dollars and fifty cents said that they felt more pain relief than when they - How Luxury Brands Trick You Into Paying More for Less #luxurylifestyle #documentary: Kind: captions Language: en Imagine walking into a store and seeing a handbag that costs more than your monthly rent. The leather looks flawless, the stitching perfect, and the brand logo instantly signals status. You might wonder, is it really worth that price, or are you paying for something else entirely? Here's the truth. Luxury brands are designed to make you pay more for less, and they've perfected the science behind it. Every detail, from store layout to product scarcity, is carefully orchestrated to create desire, exclusivity, and premium pricing. Today, we'll uncover the hidden strategies that make luxury brands some of the most profitable in the world. Even when the products themselves aren't necessarily better than their cheaper alternatives, the illusion of scarcity. Step into any high-end boutique and you'll notice something immediately. Products aren't stacked everywhere. Shelves are intentionally sparse, displays are minimal, and often the most coveted items aren't available for immediate purchase. This is no coincidence. Luxury brands thrive on the psychology of scarcity. Humans naturally desire what is rare. Limited availability signals value, creating a sense of urgency. You don't just buy a product, you gain access to something exclusive. Scarcity also generates social signaling. Owning a rare item communicates wealth, taste, and status. It's why many luxury bags are sold in small quantities or only in select cities. The strategy ensures that not everyone can have it, which only increases demand, and perceived value. Some brands even go further by creating artificial scarcity, limited edition collections, seasonal exclusives, or product drops ensure that buyers feel pressure to purchase immediately. The anticipation becomes part of the experience. Waiting months for a single item can make ownership feel like an achievement, intensifying the emotional value of the product. Branding that commands respect. Luxury isn't about the material alone. It's about the story. Every product carries a narrative about heritage, craftsmanship, and cultural significance. Brands like Chanel, Gucci, and Louis Vuitton invest millions in building an image that elevates perception of a functionality. The logo itself becomes a status symbol. When you carry it, people instantly recognize prestige. Luxury brands are masters of perception management, controlling advertising, social media presence, celebrity endorsements, and collaborations to craft an aura of sophistication. Even packaging and store experience contribute to this perception. Minimalist layouts, personalized service, and high-end interiors create an environment that reinforces exclusivity, making the product feel more valuable than its material component. Price as a status symbol. High price points are not a reflection of cost. They are a marketing strategy. In luxury, the price itself signals quality and exclusivity. Unlike mass market brands that compete on affordability, luxury labels maintain steep prices to differentiate themselves. Humans associate price with value. A $3,000 handbag might not cost significantly more to produce than a $300 alternative, but the higher price makes it prestigious and desirable. Expensive products create barriers, ensuring only a select audience can access them. This exclusivity enhances appeal and reinforces the brand's elite positioning. Additionally, high pricing taps into psychological ownership. When buyers invest a significant amount, they justify the cost through emotional attachment, reinforcing brand loyalty. The product becomes more than a utility. It represents identity, success, and taste. Crafting desire through storytelling. Luxury brands are expert storytellers. Every product is positioned as a work of art, handcrafted with decades of expertise. Marketing campaigns emphasize heritage, artisanship, and innovation, even if the functional difference is negligible. A watch isn't just a tool to tell time. It's a testament to centuries of craftsmanship. A handbag isn't just a container for belongings. It's a symbol of culture, artistry, and status. By embedding meaning into their products, luxury brands justify premium prices and transform ordinary goods into emotional investments. The power of exclusive experiences. Luxury extends beyond products into experiences. VIP events, private previews, and personalized services deepen emotional attachment. Buyers feel part of an elite community, not just owners of a product. Experiences reinforce exclusivity. Attending a brand event or accessing private collections becomes part of the appeal. The product serves as a passport to status, elevating perceived value far beyond its material worth. Brands leverage this emotional connection to maintain loyalty and justify high prices, creating a cycle where consumers repeatedly invest in identity, not just utility. Artificial scarcity and waiting lists. Limited availability is a deliberate strategy. Some high demand items are produced in extremely small quantities, and waiting lists are common. This creates anticipation and dramatically heightens perceived value, making consumers feel they are part of an elite group. Scarcity ensures that the market remains selective, protecting the brand from overexposure. The fewer the items available, the more coveted they become. Waiting for an exclusive release transforms acquisition into an achievement, reinforcing the sense of privilege and status associated with ownership. In addition, limited releases often generate media buzz and social media discussions, further amplifying desire and making these products aspirational beyond the store itself. Psychological pricing tactics. Luxury brands employ sophisticated psychological pricing strategies. Odd pricing, tiered products, and subtle upselling influence perception. A $4,950 bag feels more attainable than a $5,000 bag, even though the difference is minimal. Playing directly into consumer psychology and decision-making biases. Multiple product tiers encourage consumers to upgrade by introducing ultra premium versions. Brands make standard items seem reasonable by comparison, increasing overall revenue while preserving exclusivity. Beyond pricing, limited time offers, special additions, and hidden service perks create a perception of added value, compelling buyers to act quickly and often spend more than initially planned. Limited functionality, maximum perception. Often luxury items provide little functional advantage over cheaper alternatives. A $3,000 tote may not outperform a $300 one in durability or design. The difference lies in perceived value, branding, and exclusivity. Consumers pay for identity, status, and recognition. High margins are maintained because production costs are frequently a fraction of the retail price. Yet, emotional and social value justify extreme markups, creating lifestyle and loyalty. Luxury brands don't just sell products, they sell lifestyles and identities. Personalized services, private clubs, and curated experiences foster deep customer loyalty. Owners become advocates sharing their experiences in social circles and online. The connection is emotional. Buyers aren't just investing in items, they're investing in a community, prestige, and recognition. This ensures long-term engagement and repeated purchases, reinforcing the brand's dominance and allowing prices to remain premium. Innovation while preserving prestige. Luxury brands innovate carefully. New designs, materials, or collaborations are limited in release, ensuring they maintain elite positioning. The focus isn't mass appeal, but sustaining brand prestige. Innovation also keeps the brand relevant. For example, integrating eco-friendly materials or digital personalization enhances desiraability without diluting exclusivity. By controlling innovation, luxury brands balance modern relevance with traditional prestige, ensuring that perception and scarcity continue to drive high pricing. The secret behind paying more for less. Luxury brands have mastered the art of making consumers pay far more than the intrinsic value of products. Through scarcity, branding, storytelling, psychological pricing, and exclusive experiences, they've created a world where perception outweighs functionality. Owning a luxury item is more than possession. It's a statement of status, identity, and access. By controlling supply, shaping perception, and fostering emotional connections, these brands ensure consumers pay more for less, yet perceive it as worth every penny. In the world of mass production, luxury thrives on rarity, desire, and prestige. It's a carefully engineered ecosystem where less is truly more and the product is just one piece of the value. The rest is perception, experience, and social currency. If you found this insight fascinating, make sure to comment your thoughts below and subscribe to stay updated with more stories revealing the hidden strategies of the world's most powerful brands.

- Why China Is Behind The Global Luxury Slowdown: Kind: captions Language: en China used to be the promised land for the $380 billion luxury goods business. Now it is the land of losses. Today, we're looking at why the luxury business became so dependent on China and why it's suffering now. I'm Robert Frank for inside Wealth and you know, luxury goods sales total about $380 billion a year. But last year they declined 2%. And the reason was China. Luxury lost 50 million consumers over the past few years. And that is all because they bet so big on China. But after Covid, the young Chinese stopped buying. You had an economic crisis there and more importantly, a psychological crisis among young, wealthy consumers. Youth unemployment soared to over 20%. And rather than buying stuff and status, young people wanted experiences and memories. So the whole market shifted. But there's another secret reason for why luxury is suffering in China, and that is the rise of local brands. From watches to jewelry to fashion, even supercars. China is now making its own luxury products. And because there's a new kind of nationalism in China that values Chinese brands, luxury companies are now winning over the European brands. So I want to tell you about a watch company in China called Atelier Yuen. This is by two French people that live in China, have worked in China for years. They just launched a new watch that is titanium, that's got a double sapphire crystal that is a gorgeous purple color that costs $3,500. It's taking the country by storm. A watch like that from Switzerland would cost ten times as much. And by the way, China brands are doing the same thing with supercars, with other luxury goods, and this is a big threat to the European companies. The stocks of the big luxury giants like LVMH, Richemont, caring. They all fell over 20% last year because they built so many stores in China. They were betting their future in China. And by the way, ten years ago, China accounted for half of all luxury sales in the world. They expected that would grow instead. Now China accounts for only 12% of luxury goods sales. There are two kinds of companies that are doing okay in China. The first is the very high end companies Hermes, Cucinelli, Loro Piana. Those companies sell quiet luxury and the really rich in China are still buying. The other companies that are doing well are those that market in a local language, in products that are geared toward a Chinese customer and that actually feel more like China brands, even though they may be Chanel, Dior or European brands, they feel, at least to China, luxury consumers, more like local companies.

  20. @seafortress7541: China-made products played a significant role in the decline of Nokia and other signature brands. A key strategy of the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) has been to control and eventually dominate the global market. One of their primary tactics is to mass-produce goods and flood international markets with low-cost products — a strategy made possible by their abundant and inexpensive labor force.
As these products saturate the market, established brands struggle to remain competitive. Their offerings often come at a higher price point, while Chinese alternatives provide similar — or even more — features at a fraction of the cost.
Moreover, Chinese manufacturers are quick to load their devices with a variety of features to attract consumers. By the time a well-known brand is ready to release its next model with updated capabilities, it has often already been outpaced by Chinese brands that offer more for less.
This overwhelming influx of cheap, feature-rich products creates an uneven playing field, especially for countries and companies that lack the capacity for mass production on the same scale. It raises serious questions about fair competition and long-term sustainability in global markets.
This unfair trade also KILLS LOCAL MANUFACTURERS and LOCAL PRODUCTS.

- Luxury Shopping is OUT OF CONTROL.. Let's Unpack The 'Hermes Game': Kind: captions Language: en The Hermes game is literally so embarrassing. >> This is a Birkin 35 and I think that I'm going to sell her. >> over $25,000 trying to play this Hermes game to get a Birkin or a Kelly. I've not been offered one yet. >> a Birkin from Hermes today? No, I didn't. Oh, why is that? Same story as always. They don't have any bags. Wish they told me. There's a store where the most coveted items aren't displayed, prices aren't advertised, and the only way to buy is to be chosen. This is the world of Hermes. You don't just walk in and buy a bag like it's a latte. You enter a game, a game with no written rules and absolutely no guarantees. The Birkin and Kelly are earned through a ritual of relationship, strategy, and patience. Today we're unpacking and deep diving into the mysterious Hermes bag game. Hi babes, my name's Tarja, mother of twins, lover of luxe things, and your guide for showing up with style and intention. With the end of the year approaching, I have been seeing loads of videos about luxury purchase regrets, in particular regrets over buying Hermes bags, and I thought it would be interesting for us to discuss all of the background behind how you actually get your hands on an Hermes bag and the Hermes game and all that it entails. First though, I want you to show my hand. And so [clears throat] my hand is that I actually want an Hermes bag. I don't own one. However, I have two on my wish list, right? I have a Birkin 25, yes, unpredictable, and a mini Kelly, preferably in a really summery color. So, I'm not coming from the perspective of somebody who doesn't really understand luxury shopping or doesn't really do luxury shopping. I love shopping, I love fashion, and one of my most favorite things to do is buy expensive stuff, guys. It's It's something that I like to do, okay? So, I wouldn't say I'm a part of the Hermes online community, but I have for years followed the conversations around Hermes shopping and what it takes to get a Birkin, Kelly, and even a Constance sometimes. I also was not born rich. I am very financially literate and I'm not crazy. However, I do still want one. So, when people discuss the Hermes game, what they are referring to is the most common approach that is taken to buying some of Hermes' most notable bags. - 7 Signs Someone is Secretly Wealthy: Kind: captions Language: es-MX La mayoría de los millonarios y multimillonarios son completamente invisibles. Probablemente estén sentados junto a ti en Starbucks, y ni siquiera sabes que tienen siete, ocho o quizá hasta nueve cifras en el banco. En mi opinión, hay siete señales reveladoras que delatan si alguien es rico en secreto o no, así que si sabes qué buscar, podrás encontrarlos. Sinceramente creo que si logras adoptar algunos de sus hábitos, podría ser la clave para construir una verdadera riqueza por tu cuenta. La primera señal que vamos a ver es que la gente rica evita a toda costa hablar de las tres E. Ahora, estas tres E se refieren a ganar, a lo caro y al exceso. Por la primera E, que es ganar, nunca hablan del dinero que están ganando porque para ellos, si alguien siempre está hablando de dinero y de cuánto gana, demuestra que esa persona todavía está en la etapa de querer probar algo. Los verdaderamente ricos no tienen nada que probar porque en secreto ya saben que tienen el dinero y no hay necesidad de decírselo a nadie. La segunda E: no presumen su última compra cara. Mientras otros publican bolsas de diseñador o relojes de lujo en redes, los ricos en secreto callan sus adquisiciones. Porque si alguien habla constantemente de cuánto costó su carro, de lo caras que fueron sus vacaciones, es una clara señal de que estiraron su presupuesto para poder pagarlo y están tratando de maximizar cada dólar que gastaron obteniendo algo de fama en redes sociales o quizá contándoselo a sus amigos. Y la tercera E es que evitan vivir en exceso, al menos en cuanto a su imagen externa. La gente rica entiende que los verdaderos lujos en la vida son tener opciones, comodidad y libertad, y no tener las cosas más ostentosas. Si alguna vez te encuentras con una persona que habla de cuánto gana, cuál fue la compra más cara que ha hecho recientemente, o simplemente vive en exceso y lo presume, puedes apostar que esa persona probablemente no es rica. ¿Te crees mucho por tu Range Rover? Pues sí. Al menos yo tengo carro. ¿Tú siquiera tienes uno? Quédate aquí. ¿Quién es el plebeyo ahora? ¡¿Quién es el plebeyo ahora?! Eso pensé. Okay. Entonces, eso significa lo contrario. Los que son callados, son ricos en secreto, y creo que si puedes adoptar este tipo de actitud en tu vida, podría ayudarte a generar más riqueza. Porque la energía hipotética que gastarías en las apariencias, o en impresionar a otros, podría redirigirse a construir tu patrimonio neto real. La señal número dos de que alguien es rico en secreto: conduce un auto confiable, de cinco a más de diez años, ya pagado. No manejan autos ostentosos como BMW de alta gama, Porsches, Rolls-Royces o Lamborghinis. Eso es porque los ricos saben lo brutal de la depreciación, así que tirar quinientos a ochocientos dólares al mes en la mensualidad de un carro por algo que pierde valor cada día es un retorno de inversión muy negativo. Y si haces cuentas, podrías invertir de seis mil a nueve mil seiscientos dólares anuales en su lugar, y en diez años con rendimientos compuestos, eso bien podría valer más de cien mil. Además, un auto de cinco a más de diez años ya perdió la mayor parte de su valor por depreciación. Las curvas de depreciación de autos varían, pero se aplanan drásticamente tras cinco años. Esto significa que la mayor parte de la depreciación ya ha ocurrido, por lo que el valor se estabiliza. Si quieres ahorrar dinero en cuanto a carros, creo que tiene mucho sentido comprar un carro usado con unas treinta mil millas o quizá de tres a cinco años de uso. Ese suele ser el punto ideal entre valor y depreciación, pero si solo quieres ser una persona rica que pasa desapercibida, quizá optarías por algo más viejo. Pueden ver en esta gráfica que estos son los modelos de los carros, y el "aquí" que se indica en la gráfica es el punto de inflexión alrededor del año seis o siete, y es cuando la depreciación realmente se desacelera. Los ricos en secreto entienden que un auto es solo transporte. Prefieren ese dinero extra en inversión, evitando pagos de auto y destinándolo a activos que generen riqueza. En otras palabras, prefieren ser ricos que solo parecer ricos y al mismo tiempo deber un montón de dinero por un carro. La señal número tres de que alguien es rico en secreto es que el dinero que sí gastan lo invierten en cosas que la gente normal ni siquiera consideraría. gastan su dinero en cosas poco comunes, por ejemplo, para tener más tiempo, pueden tener un pasatiempo de coleccionables raros, o gastan dinero en pequeños lujos o comodidades de las que no te enterarías a menos que, por alguna razón, pudieras pasar veinticuatro/siete con ellos todos los días. Hablemos primero del tiempo. Una persona rica entiende que el único recurso finito en todo el mundo y en la vida es el tiempo. Por lo tanto, podrían comprar su tiempo de vuelta y están dispuestos a gastar una cantidad desproporcionada de dinero en las cosas que pueden devolverles ese tiempo. En viajes, podrían volar en privado, evitando seguridad, saliendo cuando quieran y saltándose el tedioso abordaje y desembarque. Para la comida, un chef privado les hace las compras y cocina saludable, ahorrándoles incontables horas semanales. Y las tareas del hogar, también las delegan. Para eso, seguro tienen mucha ayuda. Y en todos estos casos de ahorro de tiempo, realmente no tendrías idea de que alguien gasta dinero en estos servicios a menos que pudieras observar a esta persona por un período prolongado. La siguiente categoría de gasto en la que una persona rica podría derrochar, es en pasatiempos poco comunes o caros como coleccionar arte raro, vinos finos, recuerdos deportivos o quizá hasta tarjetas de Pokémon. En lugar de mostrar su riqueza como una persona normal, prefieren hacerlo con una apreciación por el lujo y artículos raros. Poseer algo que cientos de miles ya tienen no es lo suficientemente genial para ellos. Necesitan sentirse únicos; cuanto más raro el artículo, más codiciado. En mi opinión, es naturaleza humana. Cubiertas las necesidades básicas, los ricos ya no compiten con otros. Están tratando de diferenciarse por completo de todos los demás, y una de las formas en que lo hacen es coleccionando bienes raros o quizá comprando su tiempo de vuelta, que es su activo más... eh, valioso. Perdón, tenía que meter el comercial sutilmente. Bueno, continuemos. La señal número cuatro de que alguien es rico en secreto, es que son conscientes de su imagen. Ya aludí un poco a esto en la primera señal, pero la gente rica entiende que, por lo general, la gente siente envidia de lo que no tiene. Incluso si tu mejor amigo está feliz por ti el noventa y nueve por ciento de las veces que tienes éxito, siempre habrá un pequeño pensamiento en su mente, quizá en el fondo, de "¿Cómo sería si mi vida fuera como la de ellos?" o "¿Por qué no pude ser yo?". Esa es la naturaleza humana. Así que, para no incomodar a nadie, evitan hablar de cualquier cosa que pueda llevar a alguien a entender, por deducción, cuánto dinero podrían tener o cuán exitosos son. Nick, no puedes pagarlo. ¿Tu familia es realmente rica? Cómodamente. Eso es exactamente lo que diría una persona súper rica. Y de hecho, esta es una historia real que me pasó. Estaba comiendo con un individuo que vale más de nueve cifras, o sea, al menos cien millones de dólares de patrimonio neto. Y cuando hablaba de sus amigos en la comida, todos multimillonarios, soltó algo en la conversación muy casualmente. Fue algo como: "Soy pobre en comparación con todos mis amigos". Digo, eso es cierto para casi todos en el mundo a menos que seas Elon Musk, claro pero sigue siendo una locura pensar que alguien en el Top 400 de Forbes, por ejemplo, todavía podría compararse con personas que están más arriba en la lista. La comparación está tan arraigada en la naturaleza humana. La gente rica quiere ser cuidadosa con su imagen externa también por razones de seguridad. Si son constantemente ostentosos, o le dicen a la gente cuál es su patrimonio neto, solo están atrayendo atención negativa y resentimiento de otros, lo que podría comprometer su seguridad. La quinta señal de que una persona es rica en secreto es que hace preguntas detalladas sobre compras de menos de cien dólares, pero no pestañea ante gastos grandes y necesarios. Esto es bastante común. Podrías encontrar a una persona rica pasando de veinte a treinta minutos investigando la mejor batería portátil de veinte dólares en Amazon. Leen reseñas, se clavan viendo videos de YouTube, pero de repente, los verás soltar quince mil dólares en un techo nuevo sin pestañear. La diferencia es que la compra de la batería portátil es un gusto, mientras que el techo era una necesidad. Probablemente hay quince opciones diferentes de baterías portátiles que hacen lo mismo, así que la persona rica se toma su tiempo para encontrar el mejor valor por su dinero. El reemplazo del techo de quince mil dólares, por otro lado, es una necesidad, y especialmente si el reemplazo es urgente, simplemente gastarán el dinero para acelerar esa reparación. Sin embargo, la gente rica entiende que los grandes gastos necesarios ocurrirán, y cuando esas cosas surgen, tienen que actuar rápida y decisivamente. La señal número seis de que alguien es rico en secreto: ha dominado la espera. Generalmente es el proveedor o el vendedor de dicho artículo. Entonces, una buena lección aquí es que si estás pensando en una compra grande, esperes al menos de siete a treinta días, realmente tienes que hacer una pausa y preguntarte si de verdad seguirás queriendo ese artículo, digamos, dentro de un mes. Y quizá podrías pensar en artículos sustitutos que cumplan el mismo propósito y que no te cuesten un dineral. A veces, debes renunciar a lo que quieres ahora por una vida financiera más sólida y un mejor futuro. La mayoría no puede postergar la gratificación ni veinticuatro horas, mucho menos cinco o diez años, una disciplina clave para la riqueza. Los ricos en secreto son muy pacientes, esperan no solo ofertas, sino también otras cosas importantes en la vida. La señal número siete: viven en una casa modesta, muy por debajo de su capacidad de pago. Mientras la mayoría se estira con hipotecas apenas pagables, los ricos en secreto hacen lo opuesto. Viven en casas que parecen casi demasiado pequeñas o modestas para alguien con su nivel de ingresos, o simplemente son dueños de su casa y nunca te darían a entender que viven sin hipoteca. Si te compras una casa grande y lujosa que apenas puedes pagar, no solo tendrás más metros cuadrados que decorar, sino que probablemente necesitarás limpiadores, jardineros, y si tienes alberca, un alberquero. Y una vez que tienes una casa grande, no querrás una más pequeña. Tendrás la expectativa de mantener las apariencias para vecinos, amigos y familiares, lo que implica gastar más dinero para mantener tu estilo de vida. Hay muchos niveles de riqueza. Nivel tres: cortas tu césped porque, ya retirado, tienes tanta libertad y tiempo que quieres hacerlo tú mismo, por puro placer. - The Psychology of Rich People Who Act Broke: How This Builds Fortunes!: Kind: captions Language: en Picture this. It is Saturday morning at a gas station in suburban Omaha. A man in his 70s pulls up in a 20-year-old sedan. The paint is faded. The bumper has a small dent. He fills the tank, pays in cash, and drives away. You would never guess this man is worth over $100 billion. His name is Warren Buffett, and he could buy that entire gas station with pocket change. Yet here he is driving a car that most people would have traded in a decade ago wearing a suit from three years back about to grab breakfast at McDonald's for $3.17. [music] This is not poverty. This is not even frugality in the traditional sense. This is calculated [music] invisibility and it is how the truly wealthy stay wealthy. The rich who act broke are not playing a game of humility. They are playing a completely different game than everyone else. While you are upgrading your car to [music] match your promotion, they are driving the same Honda for 15 years. While you are buying designer clothes to signal success, they are wearing the same gray t-shirt every single day. And the gap between your net worth and theirs [music] grows wider every single month. Today, we are going to dissect the psychology behind this phenomenon. We are going to look at the numbers that prove why acting rich keeps you poor [music] and acting broke builds generational wealth. And we are going to talk about why this makes so many people deeply, viscerally uncomfortable. Because the millionaire next door does not want you to know they are a millionaire. And there is a reason for that. Let me introduce you to a character I call the invisible millionaire. You have met this person. You just did not know it. They are the guy in your office who brown bags lunch every day while you hit the beastro across the street for $15. They are the woman driving a 10-year-old Camry in the parking lot full of leased BMWs. They are the couple who politely declined the group vacation to Tulum because they would rather stay home. You think they cannot afford it? You might even pity them. Meanwhile, their net worth is seven figures and climbing. Research from Fidelity Investments found that 86% of millionaires describe themselves as disciplined and frugal, not extravagant, not flashy, frugal. 60% live in homes worth less than $500,000. 93% use coupons when they shop and are not embarrassed about it. The average millionaire spends $412 per month on groceries. The average non-millionaire American family spends $647. That is 57% more. Think about that. The people with [music] the money spend less. The people without the money spend more. This is not an accident. This is the entire game. The invisible millionaire understands something fundamental about wealth. Wealth is not what you show. Wealth is what you keep. Every dollar you spend proving you are successful is a dollar that cannot compound into actual [music] success. And here is where it gets uncomfortable. When you see someone living below their means, your brain does not compute wealth. Your brain computes failure. Because we have been socially conditioned to equate consumption with status and status with success. The invisible millionaire triggers cognitive [music] dissonance. They are winning a game you did not even know existed while you are still trying to keep up with the Joneses. and the Joneses are bankrupt. There is a reason this bothers you so much. It is called hedonic adaptation and it is ruining your financial life. Here is how it works. You get a raise. You feel amazing for about 3 weeks. Then your brain resets to baseline happiness. The new salary is the new normal. So you upgrade your lifestyle to feel that dopamine hit again. Nicer apartment, better car, expensive dinners. Your happiness returns to baseline. Your expenses stay elevated. You are now on what psychologists call the hydonic treadmill. You keep running faster, spending more, and you never get ahead because the finish line moves with you. Research shows that after positive events, people return to a relatively stable level of happiness. [music] The new car thrill dies. The bigger house becomes just your house. The luxury vacation becomes a memory. And then you need another one. The truly wealthy break this cycle. They do not get on the treadmill in the first place. Warren Buffett has been eating McDonald's for breakfast for 40 years. Do you think he stopped enjoying it? No. He never upgraded his expectations, so he never needed to chase elevated experiences. His dopamine baseline stayed low. Mark Zuckerberg wears the same outfit everyday. When asked why, he said he wants to minimize trivial decisions to preserve mental energy for important matters. Translation: He removed the psychological need to signal status through clothing, which removed the expense and the anxiety that comes with it. - "How Your Clothes Change Everything | The Power of First Impressions": Kind: captions Language: en Psychologist John Mallaloy conducted an intriguing experiment in the bustling streets of New York. He approached strangers and said, "I lost my wallet. Could you spare some change for the bus?" When he wore a suit without a tie, he earned $1 in an hour. But when he wore a suit with a high quality tie, he made $34 in the same amount of time. That's nearly four times more money just by wearing a tie. People were more generous towards someone dressed neatly and exuding trustworthiness. Studies show that it takes just 4 seconds for someone to form a first impression of us. HR managers at big companies evaluate a candidate's first impression within 30 seconds of a job interview. Showing our true skills and personality in such a short time is nearly impossible. Inevitably, people judge us based on our appearance. Research suggests that attractive individuals are 20% more likely to get a call back after an interview, receive promotions more often, earn more respect at work, and even make 5 to 15% higher salaries on average. Good looks lead people to assume you have a great personality and strong work abilities, too. Moreover, how we dress doesn't just affect how others see us. It influences our own mindset as well. Professor Adam Golinsky from Columbia University conducted a fascinating experiment. He had participants wear white lab coats and split them into two groups. One group was told, "This is a doctor's coat." While the other was told, "This is a painters smock." Then they performed a specially designed task. The group who thought they were wearing a doctor's coat showed higher focus and concentration. What you believe you're wearing directly impacts your behavior and thinking. The version of me in a hoodie might act and think differently from the version of me in a suit. The good news, about 95% of your appearance comes from your clothing and posture, things you can change if you want to. Try fixing your posture. - Should you trust your first impression? - Peter Mende-Siedlecki: Kind: captions Language: en Imagine you're at a football game when this obnoxious guy sits next to you. He's loud, he spills his drink on you, and he makes fun of your team. Days later, you're walking in the park when suddenly it starts to pour rain. Who should show up at your side to offer you an umbrella? The same guy from the football game. Do you change your mind about him based on this second encounter, or do you go with your first impression and write him off? Research in social psychology suggests that we're quick to form lasting impressions of others based on their behaviors. We manage to do this with little effort, inferring stable character traits from a single behavior, like a harsh word or a clumsy step. Using our impressions as guides, we can accurately predict how people are going to behave in the future. Armed with the knowledge the guy from the football game was a jerk the first time you met him, you might expect more of the same down the road. If so, you might choose to avoid him the next time you see him. That said, we can change our impressions in light of new information. Behavioral researchers have identified consistent patterns that seem to guide this process of impression updating. On one hand, learning very negative, highly immoral information about someone typically has a stronger impact than learning very positive, highly moral information. So, unfortunately for our new friend from the football game, his bad behavior at the game might outweigh his good behavior at the park. Research suggests that this bias occurs because immoral behaviors are more diagnostic, or revealing, of a person's true character. Okay, so by this logic, bad is always stronger than good when it comes to updating. Well, not necessarily. Certain types of learning don't seem to lead to this sort of negativity bias. When learning about another person's abilities and competencies, for instance, this bias flips. It's actually the positive information that gets weighted more heavily. Let's go back to that football game. If a player scores a goal, it ultimately has a stronger impact on your impression of their skills than if they miss the net. The two sides of the updating story are ultimately quite consistent. Overall, behaviors that are perceived as being less frequent are also the ones that people tend to weigh more heavily when forming and updating impressions, highly immoral actions and highly competent actions. So, what's happening at the level of the brain when we're updating our impressions? Using fMRI, or functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging, researchers have identified an extended network of brain regions that respond to new information that's inconsistent with initial impressions. These include areas typically associated with social cognition, attention, and cognitive control. Moreover, when updating impressions based on people's behaviors, activity in the ventrolateral prefrontal cortex and the superior temporal sulcus correlates with perceptions of how frequently those behaviors occur in daily life. In other words, the brain seems to be tracking low-level, statistical properties of behavior in order to make complex decisions regarding other people's character. It needs to decide is this person's behavior typical or is it out of the ordinary? In the situation with the obnoxious-football-fan-turned-good-samaritan, your brain says, "Well, in my experience, pretty much anyone would lend someone their umbrella, but the way this guy acted at the football game, that was unusual." And so, you decide to go with your first impression. There's a good moral in this data: your brain, and by extension you, might care more about the very negative, immoral things another person has done compared to the very positive, moral things, but it's a direct result of the comparative rarity of those bad behaviors. We're more used to people being basically good, like taking time to help a stranger in need. In this context, bad might be stronger than good, but only because good is more plentiful. Think about the last time you judged someone based on their behavior, especially a time when you really feel like you changed your mind about someone. Was the behavior that caused you to update your impression something you'd expect anyone to do, or was it something totally out of the ordinary?

- The Psychology of First Impressions: Kind: captions Language: en When you meet someone for the very first time, before they even speak, before they so much as smile, your brain has already formed a judgment. Within seconds, maybe even milliseconds, you've decided if they're trustworthy, competent, likable, or worth your time. This automatic process is what psychologists call the first impression. And it's one of the most powerful forces in human interaction. So, why does this happen so fast? And can we ever truly recover from a bad first impression? Let's dive into the psychology of first impressions. Our brains are wired for survival. For early humans, the ability to quickly judge whether someone was a threat or an ally was crucial. This survival mechanism is still with us today. When we encounter a new face, our brains process it using a region called the amydala, known for its role in detecting threat, and the fusififor face area, which specializes in facial recognition. Within 1/10enth of a second, we're already labeling someone as warm or cold, strong or weak, friend or foe. But here's the catch. First impressions are often based more on intuition than information. They rely heavily on appearance, body language, tone of voice, and even context. Are they well-dressed? Do they make eye contact? Do they smile? Do they interrupt? We may believe we're being fair or rational, but studies show that our first impressions are influenced by biases, many of which we're not even aware of. Things like height, posture, race, gender, and attractiveness, can all skew how we perceive someone. This is what psychologists refer to as implicit bias. It's not always malicious, but it's real, and it affects our decisions, even when we think we're being objective. What's more, first impressions tend to be sticky. Once we've made up our mind about someone, we're more likely to interpret their future behavior in a way that confirms our original belief. This is called the confirmation bias. If you decide someone is confident, you'll likely see their actions through that lens. If you believe someone is untrustworthy, you may dismiss or downplay their positive qualities. We don't just make snap judgments, we defend them, sometimes without realizing it. Now, let's flip the perspective. What are people noticing about you when they meet you for the first time? Research tells us that people are particularly sensitive to two traits in a first impression. Warmth and competence. Warmth answers the question, "Can I trust you?" Competence answers, "Can I respect you?" These two traits work together to form the foundation of how we're perceived. Too much competence without warmth, you might come off as intimidating. Too much warmth without competence, you may be liked but not taken seriously. So, how do you make a strong first impression? It starts with presence. Being fully engaged, making eye contact, offering a genuine smile, and showing interest in the other person. These are subtle cues that signal warmth. Meanwhile, posture, clarity of speech, and confidence in your movements can convey competence. You don't have to be perfect, just intentional. But here's the good news. First impressions can be changed. They're powerful, yes, but not permanent. Especially when we have repeated exposure to someone. As we gather more information, our initial judgments can evolve. Sometimes the people we like the least at first become our closest allies. And sometimes the people we trust right away disappoint us. The real magic happens when we slow down, question our assumptions, and give others the space to show who they really are beyond the first few seconds. Because ultimately, while first impressions may open the door, it's our ongoing behavior, character, and consistency that determine the relationship that follows. So the next time you meet someone new or worry about how you came across, remember this. You're not just meeting them. You're meeting your own expectations, your own instincts, and your own story about what people are like. The more aware you are of that process, the more power you have to shape it for yourself and for others. First impressions may be fast, but wisdom takes its time.

- Why Rich People Don't Wear Luxury: Kind: captions Language: en Louis Vuitton, Dior, Saint Laurent, Gucci. These are just some of the brands belonging to major luxury conglomerates LVMH and Kering. Positioned at the forefront of the global luxury market, these conglomerates are hugely responsible for the industry's staggering 397-billion-dollar market valuation. From clothing and handbags to jewellery and watches, these conglomerates, alongside a few independent houses, stand as the driving forces behind the most coveted and elite luxury products. This industry is a powerhouse. Just to provide you with further insight, LVMH CEO Bernard Arnault, whose estimated net worth is 200-billion-dollars, made his first appearance on the Forbes billionaires list in 1997. The conglomerateur briefly even claimed the title of the richest person alive in April, surpassing tech giant Elon Musk. But who exactly makes up the clientele of these huge conglomerates? Who shoulders the weight of that giant 397-billion-dollar valuation? Is it the elite? The upper class? Surprisingly, no. It’s predominantly the upper-middle class that sustains the industry. Jean-Jacques Guiony, the Chief Financial Officer at LVMH, stated to the Financial Times in July, “We don't sell most Louis Vuitton products to rich people; it is to people who have money and want to indulge themselves.” So, the question becomes: why aren't the mega-rich, the upper-class, immersing themselves in this industry as enthusiastically as their middle-class counterparts? Why are they denying themselves of those sweet, sweet offerings from Louis Vuitton or Saint Laurent? Why are the 1% not buying luxury? But before we answer those questions, let's define what a luxury product actually is. I always wonder, what the process is creating these pieces. It almost looks like the universe. There is a life inside. Absolutely. While there are a lot, and I mean a lot, of definitions for what qualifies a product to be considered luxury, the most common criteria usually comes down to three things: a premium price point that creates a high barrier to entry, incredible craftsmanship with exceptional attention to detail, and a distinct capability to function as a symbol denoting wealth, or more specifically distinction. All successful brands within the realm of luxury are able to offer these services in one way or another. That being said, there is still a complex hierarchical web at play. So, let's begin at the basic level by categorizing them into financial groups. The biggest luxury houses, the ones that come to mind when most people picture luxury, fall into the category we'll refer to as the "Major League". Examples in this league include Louis Vuitton, Dior, Gucci, and Chanel. All of these brands either already have, or are on track to achieve, annual revenues of 10-billion-dollars. The standout in this group is undoubtedly LVMH's Louis Vuitton, which this year made history as the first luxury fashion brand to attain an annual revenue of 20-billion-dollars. These brands, as heads of their industry, typically lead the trends that we see both on the runway and on the streets. Their mission goes beyond merely creating luxury; they strive to push the boundaries of creativity itself. The next group of luxury houses is what we'll refer to as the "Minor League." Most luxury brands find their place in this category; but even in this league, there is a spectrum - both financial and creative. Some brands are well known to the general public, whereas others are more obscure, limited to fashion enthusiasts and people with enough money to buy luxury products. Can you please spell Gabbana? Hello? The more well-known and financially successful brands in this group generate around 2 to 5-billion-dollars in annual revenue. A few brands under this section are Celine, Saint Laurent, Armani, and Balenciaga. This subdivision in the Minor League mirrors the Major League in that, similar to most Major League brands, some are very much interested in a boundary-pushing and a more artistic vision of fashion. But it differs in that certain brands focus more on maintaining a traditional identity, aiming to conserve specific codes of dress. The other half of the Minor League is made up of brands not widely recognized by the general public but still hold prominence within the fashion industry. These brands cultivate their own niche of luxury consumers and typically generate annual revenues under 2-billion-dollars. Examples in this group include Loro Piana, Brunello Cucinelli, Bottega Veneta, and Loewe. Most of these brands fall under the category of quiet luxury - this will be important later - but some brands like Bottega Veneta and Loewe, are typically considered to be artistic rather than traditional houses. Despite this, both brands are still associated to the rigid quiet luxury movement because they offer products that align with the style’s philosophy. Both brands seem to have ambitions of transitioning to the other half of the Minor League, aiming to generate over 2-billion-dollars in revenue in the coming years and attain broader recognition beyond their niche. An example of a transformation of this type can be found in Celine. Under Hedi Slimane’s stewardship, the French house went from being a relatively niche 500-million-dollar brand to a global powerhouse raking in over 2-billion-dollars in revenue annually. Okay, now that we’ve outlined a general idea of the categories, we can begin to answer our initial question: why aren’t the 1% buying into luxury? Well, they are, but they aren’t. Remember when I said luxury categorisation is sort of like a web? This is where that idea comes into play: most rich people in America and Europe, and when I say “rich” I mean the top upper-class, opt to not invest in Major League brands when shopping for luxury goods. Instead, they spend their money on select Minor League brands, with a few exceptions that we’ll touch on later, that all fall within or intersect with the realm of quiet luxury. Quiet luxury is a fashion aesthetic coined to challenge the typical style associated to wealthy groups of people. It’s completely understated and subdued. See, there are multiple "variations" of quiet luxury but recurring motifs in most of them all include: subtle usage of or no logos, a subdued or achromatic colour palette, and no flamboyant designs. Of course, there are ways to experiment and play around with these elements and still embody the aesthetic - especially when it comes to embellishment, accessories, and aristocratic colourways such as gold and silver. But essentially, the aesthetic is about elevating "normal" clothes to offer an "if you know, you know" type of distinction. And why is this distinction important to the 1%? Sign value. See, sign value, a term French sociologist Jean Baudrillard, functions as the cornerstone of a luxury product. It delves into what an item symbolises in terms of social, cultural, or personal contexts. In the realm of luxury, sign value stands as the driving force behind the entire industry. It's the literal reason for its existence and success in the modern world. Sign value also reveals why brands situated in the Minor League, despite bringing in significantly lower annual revenue than their Major League counterparts, might possess symbolic significance equivalent to or even surpassing that of a Major League brand. This phenomenon can be attributed to factors and characteristics such as exclusivity, heritage, and craftsmanship, that become intertwined with the brand's identity. So by choosing to dress in a more understated manner, the 1% adopt a kind of sign value that signals to others that they aren't new money - people who have only recently acquired their wealth. The idea is that new money tends to showcase affluence through flashy displays, often marked by ostentation and conspicuous consumption. In contrast, the 1% tend to prefer a subtler approach. Embodying a more restrained sense of style that suggests they come from longstanding generational wealth. This distinction reflects a cultural narrative where old money conveys a sense of tradition, refinement, and social standing. While new money is associated with a more brash and immediate display of wealth. That said, despite being considered new money because their wealth doesn’t go back generations, members of the LVMH family, who are literally at the helm of almost every major luxury brand in the world, also embrace a simplistic approach to style. This underscores two points. First, that Old Money-New Money dynamics are less literal than they are a state of mind; and second, that these rigid distinctions do not completely apply to the modern day. Increasingly, uber-rich families and individuals who would be considered new money are adopting characteristics traditionally associated with old money. But back to the topic at hand, the 1% don't want Louis Vuitton monograms covering their handbags, and they really don’t want Gucci motifs all over their clothing. They intentionally avoid being plastered in overt luxury because they’re aiming to convey that they themselves personify wealth. I hinted at this before, but the brands that capture the attention of the 1% can usually find themselves in the Minor League. Brunello Cucinelli, Loro Piana, Ralph Lauren, Celine. All these brands fit neatly into the realm of quiet luxury. See, It's not the uber-rich carrying the heavyweights of the industry, most of them purposefully ignore the big designers and brands in fashion. it's the upper-middle class doing all that heavy lifting. And how do we know this? During the pandemic, while most businesses were grappling with dangerous financial underperformance and the very real possibility of permanent closures, the fashion industry thrived. LVMH's fashion and leather goods units saw a significant 18% increase in sales during the fourth quarter of 2020. Gucci, Kering’s crown jewel, delivered revenues of 11-billion-dollars, up 31.2% from 2020, exceeding pre-pandemic levels in 2021. Another luxury conglomerate, Richemont, reported sales exceeding 6 billion dollars in the company's third quarter of 2021, a performance 38% better than their 2019 Christmas quarter before the pandemic. The industry was experiencing an unprecedented boom. Any guess who was leading this boom? The upper-middle class of course. You see, during the pandemic a significant portion of the upper-middle class indulged in luxury treats with the additional savings they had in the bank. There was this sense that the world could literally come to an end at any moment. And this uncertainty about the future led many to believe that living in the moment was imperative. That even when quarantining with only a few friends and family, we had to live every day like it’s our last. And what was the result of this YOLO attitude? People started splurging out. You need to keep in mind what our good friend Jean-Jacques Guiony said earlier. Most luxury items from Major League brands are not targeted at the uber-wealthy upper classes, but instead at the upper middle classes. While I should note that Major League brands do offer custom made-to-order products exclusively designed for the 1% to request and purchase, such as couture and tailor-made clothing, the majority of their offerings cater to and are marketed towards the upper-middle class and “nouveau riche”. Are they expensive? I mean, yeah. But are they within reach? Relying solely on selling to the 1% wouldn't be enough to pass that 10-billion-dollar mark. It's important to know that when people say the “1%” nowadays, it's usually colloquialism for the 0.1%. A very, very, small demographic of people in the world. And do you think ambitious brands and conglomerates want to depend solely, or even primarily, on this demographic to achieve 10-billion-dollar goals? No way. It’s too risky. But the middle classes on the other hand? They’re the perfect group to rely on. Estimates indicate that the middle class demographic comprised about 350 million people. More than the entire population of the US. Men and women from the middle class are the ones purchasing those 3000-dollar handbags. ones splurging on pieces from the latest fashion week collections. They have the financial flexibility to occasionally indulge themselves without compromising their overall financial health. They are the literal lifeline of the modern-day fashion market. And where do the middle class shop when they want to indulge in luxury? The Major League of course. And when you think about it, it makes sense. Most people, even if they don't openly acknowledge it, place high significance on sign value. Only a small percentage of people make luxury purchases without considering the symbolic value attached to that particular luxury item. Imagine you don't have the financial means to consistently splurge on luxury. Instead, you opt to invest in a select few pieces of expensive luxury goods every few months or so. In this scenario, are you more likely to choose a product that clearly signifies its high price point, or would you rather something more obscure, a logo-less handbag for example, only recognizable to a specific niche that you’ll probably never run into? Chances are, you would lean towards the more “obvious” bag because it'll be easier to rationalise - and you’ve probably been conditioned to like it more anyway. How with this product which people want, can we maximise the desire. Our business – you asked a question about luxury – luxury, for me, is how can you create desire. This is why most Major League luxury brands become less attractive to the elite upper classes who seek exclusivity. They instead gravitate towards more understated brands. Still, for conglomerates, it's all the same. Their market value and revenue increase regardless, and they often have other brands in their portfolio that cater to the needs of both the 1% and the middle class. But remember, that was life during the pandemic. During that epic boom with constant, record-breaking highs in the industry. But what about now? Yeah, now, it’s not looking so good. The fashion industry, across nearly every metric, has been struggling to grapple with the post-pandemic world. Although still generating billions, the industry has significantly slowed down this past year, with LVMH experiencing a below-estimated 9% organic revenue growth and being dethroned by drugmaker Novo Nordisk as the most valuable company in Europe. Major League brands like Gucci are going through a very public struggle, with the brand's organic sales falling 7% in the third quarter of 2023 - worse than the 6.2% expected by analysts. The brand even appointed new creative director, Sabato De Sarno, who critics have noted has taken a considerably less daring approach to Gucci than his predecessor Alessandro Michele. Creative brands in the Minor League, such as Saint Laurent and Balenciaga, have also had to grapple with the new reality, with both reportedly experiencing some level of underperformance in sales this year. But why is this? Why did an industry experiencing so much growth during a pandemic of all of a sudden begin to slow down once we emerged from the crisis? Simple. The 1% are still spending, and the middle classes are not. Middle-class populations have begun to cut back on luxury spending, opting instead to save more and uniformly limit expenditures on non-essentials. They’re also delaying major purchases such as homes and cars, redirecting more money into savings amid rising levels of inflation and a global cost-of-living crisis. Bloomberg reports that this shift in behaviour is occurring on a global scale, affecting China, America, and Europe – all massive markets for the luxury industry. This trend explains the declines and underperformances seen from the Major League brands relying on middle-class spending. But, as you’ve probably already guessed, the brands not recording underperformances are predominantly from that latter category of the Minor League. Brunello Cucinelli, Ermenegildo Zegna, Loro Piana, Loewe. None of these brands have faltered in the post-pandemic era. In fact, some of them even reported record earnings. Brunello Cucinelli for example not only jumped a solid 27.5% in revenues during the second quarter of 2023 but also upgraded their revenue forecast for the third time last October because of this immense growth. Because these brands are attractive to the billionaire population, who are unfazed by concerns of inflation or costs of living, they continue to ride the money wave while their big-brother brands struggle to navigate the oceans. In addition to the Minor League brands appealing to the 1%, there are also a few Major League anomalies thriving in the post-pandemic landscape. One of these anomalies is French luxury brand Hermès. Despite undoubtedly being a Major League player, the house has achieved insane financial success, raking in 12-billion-dollars in annual revenue last year. Their ability to preserve a level of selectiveness gives them sign value that’s hard to find in most Major League fashion brands. Sign value that’s usually only reserved for the niche Minor League brands. With all that being said, quiet luxury doesn't appear to be so quiet anymore. TikTok videos, Instagram posts, and general discourse about "quiet luxury," "old money," "stealth wealth," and other variations of its kind have been on the rise for a while now. This surge has prompted people from all demographics to try to emulate the style. Whether that’s the small minority of the upper-middle class that still sporadically indulge in luxury switching to more niche brands, or the broader population opting for fast-fashion dupes in an attempt to mirror the 1%. Remember when I mentioned that Brunello Cucinelli and some other Minor League brands were achieving record sales? This is undoubtedly because some in the middle class, who are willing to take on a risk and splurge, may be shifting their preferences towards this "new" sign value. You just can't help but wonder: Is it just a matter of time before we witness a resistance of this style among the uber-rich? Could they abandon minimalism for something more dramatic in reaction to the once "quiet" style no longer maintaining its hushed demeanour? Only time will tell.
